<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{assump:obs-sample}
    Assume the i.i.d.~super-population model with SUTVA and unconfoundedness, so that $\{Y(1),Y(0)\}\perp \!\!\! \perp Z{\,|\,} X$ under $\mathbb{P}$, and $Y_i=Y_i(Z_i)$. 
    In addition, suppose $X$ has marginal density $f$ with respect to the Lebesgue measure on $\Omega=[0,1]^d$. Suppose $f(\cdot)$ and $e(\cdot)$ have $C^1$ extensions to
a neighborhood of $\Omega$. For fixed 
constants $\underline f,\overline f,\eta_0,\eta_1$, we have 
$0<\underline f\le f(x)\le\overline f<\infty$ and  
$\eta_0\le e(x)\le\tfrac12-\eta_1$,
for all $x\in\Omega$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The super-population model used in this paper follows that of~\cite{GR2023} but differs from some common alternatives in the literature, such as the design-based inference where $\{(X_i,Y_i(1),Y_i(0))\}_{i=1}^n$ are viewed as fixed and the sole source of randomness is the treatment assignment. In that regime, the randomness in the matching design arises from the treatment assignment only, and the fixed covariate values makes the analysis even more challenging. In contrast, the super-population model enables the analysis of large-sample behavior via concentration inequalities under transparent conditions. Our setting ensures $n_0$ and $n_1$ are of the same order, in contrast to the requirements in~\cite{AI2012} that $n_1^r/n_0=O(1)$ for some $r>d$ so the control reservoir grows much faster than the $n_1$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
When $A_0=I$, the objective~\eqref{eq:def_matching_obj} is the total squared Euclidean distance. When $A_0$ is the (empirical) inverse covariance matrix, the objective is the total squared Mahalanobis distance. This differs from the Mahalanobis norm objective in \cite{GR2023}, but enables principled theoretical analysis and is broadly used in practice~\citep{bind2019bridging,zhang2023matching}.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
We close the section by stating the notations used throughout this paper. 
For a vector $v$, we denote it Euclidean norm by $\|v\|$. For a matrix
$M$, let
$\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{\|v\|=1}\|Mv\|$
be its operator norm; when $M$ is symmetric,
$\lambda_{\min}(M)$ and $\lambda_{\max}(M)$ denote its smallest
and largest eigenvalues. We write $I_d$ for the $d\times d$
identity matrix. For a scalar function $g$ on a set $S$, let
$\|g\|_S=\sup_{x\in S}|g(x)|$, which is also written as $\|g\|_\infty$ when no confusion arises. The gradient and Hessian of $g$ are denoted by
$\nabla g$ and $\nabla^2 g$, respectively. For a square-integrable
real random variable $W$, we denote its $L_2$-norm as 
$\|W\|_2=\{\mathbb{E}(W^2)\}^{1/2}$.
We use $\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{\cdot\}$ for an indicator and
$x_+=\max\{x,0\}$ for the positive part.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:observational}
Suppose  $d\le3$, and Assumption~\ref{assump:obs-sample} holds. 
Let $\hat{A}$ be a data-dependent symmetric matrix such that $||\hat{A}-A_0||_{\textnormal{op}} = O_P(1/\sqrt{n})$ for some fixed symmetric positive-definite matrix $A_0$. 
On the event $\mathcal{I}_n$, let
$\hat\sigma_n\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}$ be a measurable injection with optimization gap  
$\xi_n = Q_n(\hat\sigma_n;\hat{A})
-\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}}Q_n(\sigma;\hat{A}) = o_P(1/n)$. 
Then for every
fixed $L\in(0,\infty)$, as $n\to \infty$, 
\@\label{eq:observational-balance}
\sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}
|B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(g)| = o_P(1).
\@
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
The imbalance control in~\eqref{eq:observational-balance} is stated in probability. The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:observational} (especially the intermediate result Theorem~\ref{thm:balance}) also yields stronger moment bounds for any fixed function $g\in \mathcal{F}_L$ 
and exact optimizer under a fixed symmetric positive definite matrix $A_0$. 
                        
                                                    
                           
                                                  
       
For all sufficiently large $n$, recalling that $\sigma_n^\star$ is the exact optimizer, we have 
\[
\left|\mathbb{E} [B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)]\right|
\le C_g\Gamma_n,
\qquad
\mathbb{E}\!\left[B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)^2\right]
\le C_g\Big(\Gamma_n^2+\frac{\Gamma_n}{\sqrt n}\Big),
\qquad
\Gamma_n\asymp n^{-2/d}(\log n)^{1+2/d},
\]
where the constant $C_g$ does not depend on $n$. 
Thus, for $d\leq 3$, the imbalance $B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(g)$ is root-$n$ negligible in the $L^2$-norm. See Proposition~\ref{prop:observational-moments} for a formal statement.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:dimension-obs}
For every fixed integer $d\ge4$, there exist fixed $C^\infty$ functions $f,e$ satisfying Assumption~\ref{assump:obs-sample} and a fixed $C^\infty$ function $g$ on a neighborhood of $\Omega$, with $0\le g\le1$ and $\sup_\Omega\|\nabla g\|\le1$, such that the exact minimizer $\sigma_n^\star$ under $A_0=I_d$ has the following properties. If $d=4$, there are constants $b_*,p_*>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:iid-prob}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\{\sqrt{n_1}\,B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)\ge b_*\}\ge p_*.
\end{equation}
If $d>4$, then $\sqrt n B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)\to+\infty$ in probability. The imbalance is  set to be zero on $\mathcal I_n^c$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{assump:match}
    Suppose $d\leq 3$. 
    Let $\mathcal{G}_n = \sigma((X_i,Z_i)_{i=1}^n)$ be the $\sigma$-field used in constructing the matching. 
    Let $\hat{A}$ be a data-dependent symmetric matrix that is $\mathcal{G}_n$-measurable such that $||\hat{A}-A_0||_{\textnormal{op}} = O_P(1/\sqrt{n})$ for some fixed symmetric positive-definite matrix $A_0$. 
On the event $\mathcal{I}_n=\{2\leq n_1\leq n_0\}$, let
$\hat\sigma_n\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}$ be a $\mathcal{G}_n$-measurable injection with optimization gap  
$\xi_n = Q_n(\hat\sigma_n;\hat{A})
-\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}}Q_n(\sigma;\hat{A}) = o_P(1/n)$.
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{assump:outcome}
    The function $\mu_0$ has a $C^2$ extension near $\Omega$, $\mu_1$ is bounded and measurable, and $\sigma_0^2$ is continuous. 
    In addition, $\mathbb{E}[(Y(z)-\mu_z(x))^4{\,|\,} X=x]\leq M$ for some a common  constant $M>0$ for all $x$ and $z\in \{0,1\}$. Define $v_\tau=\operatorname{{\rm Var}}_1\{\tau(X)\}$, $v_\epsilon={\mathbb E}_1\{\sigma_1^2(X)+\sigma_0^2(X)\}$,  
and suppose $V_{\textnormal{ATT}}:=v_\tau+v_\epsilon>0$.
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:ATT}\label{thm:bootstrap}
Under Assumptions~\ref{assump:obs-sample},~\ref{assump:match}, and~\ref{assump:outcome}, as $n\to \infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ATT-main}
 \sqrt {n_1}(\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}-\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}})\Rightarrow N(0,V_{\textnormal{ATT}}),
 \qquad \hat V_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}\to V_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}\quad\text{in probability}.
\end{equation} 
Hence the Wald-CI $\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}/{n_1}}$ has limiting coverage of $1-\alpha$, where $z_{1-\alpha/2}$ is the $1-\alpha/2$-th quantile of a standard normal distribution. Furthermore, the matched-pair bootstrap satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bootstrap}
 \sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^*\{ 
 T^{*(1)}\leq x \}-\Phi(x) 
                  \right|\to 0
 \quad\text{in probability}.
\end{equation}
For bootstrap, let $B=B_n$ be a
deterministic sequence of positive integers satisfying
$B_n\to\infty$ as $n\to\infty$.
For the t-statistics in~\eqref{eq:def_bootstrap_att_tstat}, define $c_{q}^*$ as the $q$-th empirical quantile of $\{T^{*(b)}\}_{b=1}^B$. Then the bootstrap confidence interval $\mathrm{CI}_{n,1-\alpha}^{\mathrm{boot}} = [\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}} - c_{1-\alpha/2}^*  \cdot(\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ATT}}/n_1)^{1/2}, \hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}} - c_{\alpha/2}^* \cdot (\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ATT}}/n_1)^{1/2}]$ has limiting coverage of $1-\alpha$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- assumption-4: body_md -->
\label{assump:cdf}
    The functions $H_0,H_1$ are jointly measurable, and there exists a fixed $L<\infty$ such that 
 $\{H_0(y{\,|\,}\cdot):y\in\mathbb{R}\}\subseteq\mathcal F_L$.
<!-- end assumption-4 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:wald_cdf}
Under Assumptions~\ref{assump:obs-sample},~\ref{assump:match}, and~\ref{assump:cdf}, as $n\to \infty$, 
\begin{equation}\label{dist:eq:CDFCLT}
 \sqrt{n_1}(\hat F_1-F_{1,T},\hat F_0-F_{0,T})
 \Rightarrow(\mathbb B_1,\mathbb B_0)
 \quad\text{in }\ell^\infty(\mathbb R)^2.
\end{equation}
For each $y\in \mathbb{R}$, the Wald interval $\textnormal{CI}_{n,1-\alpha}^{\textnormal{contrast,Wald}}(y) = \hat{D}(y) \pm z_{1-\alpha/2} \sqrt{\hat{V}_D(y)/n_1}$ has a limiting coverage of $1-\alpha$ for $D(y)$. 
For bootstrap, suppose $B_n\to \infty$. Define
 $W_F=\max_z\sup_y|\mathbb B_z(y)|$ and 
 $W_D=\sup_y|\mathbb B_1(y)-\mathbb B_0(y)|$. 
  
If $W_F$ has a regular $(1-\alpha)$-quantile, then
\begin{equation}\label{dq:eq:joint-cdf-coverage}
 \mathbb{P}\big\{ F_{z,T}(y) \in \textnormal{CI}_{z,n,1-\alpha}^{\textnormal{joint}}
       \text{ for every }y\in\mathbb{R},\ z\in\{0,1\}\big\}
 \to 1-\alpha.
\end{equation}
If $W_D$ has a regular $(1-\alpha)$-quantile, then
\begin{equation}\label{dq:eq:direct-cdf-coverage}
 \mathbb P\{D(y)\in\mathrm{CI}^{\textnormal{contrast}}_{n,1-\alpha}(y)
                         \text{ for every }y\in\mathbb R\}
 \to1-\alpha.
\end{equation}
The coverage probabilities include both data and bootstrap sampling randomness.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- assumption-5: body_md -->
\label{assump:quantile}
    For each $z\in \{0,1\}$, there is a compact interval $J_z = [a_z,b_z]$ such that $F_{z,T}(a_z)<u_-$ and $F_{z,T}(b_z)>u_+$. On a neigborhood of $J_z$, $F_{z,T}$ is continuously differentiable and its derivative $f_{z,T}$ is bounded away from zero on $J_z$.
<!-- end assumption-5 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:quantile} 
Under Assumptions~\ref{assump:obs-sample},~\ref{assump:match},~\ref{assump:cdf} and~\ref{assump:quantile}, as $n\to \infty$, 
\begin{equation}\label{dist:eq:Qlimit}
 \sqrt{n_1}(\hat\Delta_Q-\Delta_Q)
 \Rightarrow\mathcal Z_Q
 \quad\text{in }\ell^\infty(\mathcal U),\qquad
 \mathcal Z_Q(u)=
 -\frac{\mathbb B_1\{Q_1(u)\}}{f_{1,T}\{Q_1(u)\}}
 +\frac{\mathbb B_0\{Q_0(u)\}}{f_{0,T}\{Q_0(u)\}},
\end{equation}
where the joint process $(\mathbb{B}_1,\mathbb{B}_0)$ is defined in Section~\ref{subsec:wald_cdf}. 
Suppose $B_n\to\infty$. If
$W_Q=\sup_{u\in\mathcal U}|\mathcal Z_Q(u)|$
has a regular $(1-\alpha)$-quantile, then
\begin{equation}\label{dq:eq:quantile-coverage}
 \mathbb{P}\big\{\Delta_Q(u)\in\mathrm{CI}^{Q}_{n,1-\alpha}(u) \text{ for every }u\in\mathcal{U}\big\} \to 1-\alpha.
\end{equation}
For every fixed $u\in\mathcal U$ with
$\operatorname{Var}\{\mathcal Z_Q(u)\}>0$, 
\begin{equation}\label{dq:eq:quantile-pointwise-coverage}
 \mathbb{P}\big\{\Delta_Q(u)\in\mathrm{CI}^{Q,\mathrm{pt}}_{n,1-\alpha}(u)\big\} \to 1-\alpha.
\end{equation}
The above coverage probabilities include both the data and bootstrap sampling randomness.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:rand_inf}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{assump:obs-sample},~\ref{assump:match},~\ref{assump:outcome} hold, and $B_n\to \infty$. 
Under $H_{0,\mathrm{weak}}$, for every fixed
$\alpha\in(0,1)$, 
\[
\mathbb P\{p_{{\textnormal{RI}},n}\le\alpha \}\to\alpha.
\]
The same conclusion holds under $H_{0,\mathrm{sharp}}$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:rand_boundary}
Assume $B_n\to \infty$. 
For the model \eqref{eq:rimean:binary-model}, for every fixed
$\alpha\in(0,1)$, there is a constant $\epsilon_\alpha>0$ such that
\[
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb P\{p_{{\textnormal{RI}},n}\le\alpha\}
\ge\alpha+\epsilon_\alpha.
\]
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- assumption-6: body_md -->
\label{ass:cov}
$\{T_i\}_{i=1}^{n_1}$ and $\{C_j\}_{j=1}^{n_0}$ are i.i.d.~from distributions with densities $p_1$ and $p_0$, respectively. The densities $p_0,p_1$ have $C^1$ extensions to a neighborhood of $\Omega$, satisfy $0<\underline p\le p_z(x)\le\overline p<\infty$ on $\Omega$, and have bounded first derivatives. For some fixed $\delta>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:surplus}
 n_0 p_0(x)\ge(1+\delta)n_1 p_1(x),\qquad x\in\Omega.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-6 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:uniform_balance}
    Under Assumption~\ref{ass:cov},   suppose $d\leq 3$, and the (approximate) solution $\hat\sigma_n$ satisfies $\Delta_n := Q_n(\hat\sigma_n ;A_0)-Q_n^\star = o_P(1/n)$. Then, for every fixed $0<L<\infty$, we have 
    \@\label{eq:uniform_balance}
    \sqrt{n}\sup_{g\in \mathcal{F}_L} \big| B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(g)\big| \to 0
    \@
    in probability. In particular, this conclusion holds for the exact optimizer $\sigma_n^\star$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:balance}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:cov}, for all sufficiently large $n$, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:balance-moments}
 \big| {\mathbb E} B_{D,A}(g) \big| \le C\Gamma_n,
 \qquad
 {\mathbb E} B_{D,A}(g)^2\le C\{\Gamma_n^2+\Gamma_n/\sqrt n\}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:balance-rate}
 B_{D,A}(g)=O_P \big(  
 n^{-2/d}(\log n)^{1+2/d}
 +n^{-1/4-1/d}(\log n)^{1/2+1/d} \big).
\end{equation}
For $d\le3$, $\sqrt n B_{D,A}(g)\to0$ in $L^2$ and in probability as $n\to \infty$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:unique}
For every fixed $t\in\mathbb{R}$, the minimizer in~\eqref{eq:def_Vt} is unique almost surely.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:stab_opt_value}
    If $D\in \mathcal{E}_n$ and $D'$ is a one-coordinate replacement of $D$ by any point in $\Omega$, then $|V_D(t)-V_{D'}(t)|\leq C H_n(t)/n$ uniformly for every $|t|\leq 1$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:value-bounds}
Choose ${c_{\textnormal{grid}}}$ sufficiently large. Uniformly over deterministic $|t|\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:value-bounds}
 \operatorname{{\rm Var}} \big( V_D(t)\big) \le\frac{CH_n(t)^2}{n},\qquad
 \sup_{x\in\Omega}|\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} [V_D(t)]|\le\frac{CH_n(t)}n.
\end{equation} 
Furthermore, for every deterministic \(t\in[-1,1]\)  and every measurable optimizer of $V_D(t)$, i.e., any measurable map $s_t:\Omega^n\to \mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}$ satisfying 
$s_t(D)\in \mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\sigma} 
\left\{Q_D(\sigma,A)+tB_D(\sigma,g)\right\}$ almost surely, we have 
$\mathbb{E}[L_D(s_t(D))]\leq C(r_n^2+t^2)$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:slope}
For every deterministic $|t|\le1$, $V_D'(t)$ exists almost surely, $V_D'(t)=B_D(\sigma_t,g)$ almost surely, and $|\mathbb{E} V_D'(t)|\le C\{H_n(t)+|t|\}$, where $\sigma_t$ is defined in~\eqref{eq:def_Vd_minimizer}.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:obj_gap}
    Under Assumption~\ref{ass:cov}, for any deterministic $h_n \in (0,  1/\log n]$, it holds that 
    \@\label{eq:obj_gap}
    |B_D( \hat\sigma, g) |\leq Z_{n,h_n} + \Delta_n(\hat\sigma)/h_n,\quad Z_{n,h_n} =O_P\Big(\Gamma_n +h_n + \frac{\Gamma_n}{\sqrt{n} h_n} \Big).
    \@
    Here we define $Z_{n,h}=|R_h|+|L_h|$. In particular, if $\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A) = O_P(\epsilon_n)$ for a deterministic sequence of $\epsilon_n \geq 0$ obeying $\epsilon_n = o((\log n)^{-2})$, then 
    \@\label{eq:obj_gap_2}
|B_D( \hat\sigma, g) | = O_P\Big( \Gamma_n + (\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n} + \epsilon_n)^{1/2} \Big),
    \@
    and $L_D(\hat\sigma) = O_P(r_n^2+\epsilon_n)$. 
    If $d\leq3$ and $\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A)=o_P(1/n)$, then 
    $B_D( \hat\sigma, g) = o_P(n^{-1/2})$ and $L_D(\hat\sigma) = o_P(1)$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
By definition of $V_D(t)$ and $\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A)$, we have 
\$
&V_D(h) \leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A) + h B_D( \hat\sigma, g) 
\leq V_D(0) + \Delta_{D}(\hat\sigma,A) +  h B_D( \hat\sigma, g),\\ 
&\text{and}\quad V_D(-h) \leq V_D(0) + \Delta_{D}(\hat\sigma,A) - h B_D( \hat\sigma, g).
\$
Then we have 
\$
R_h - \Delta_{D}(\hat\sigma,A)/h \leq B_D( \hat\sigma, g) \leq L_h + \Delta_{D}(\hat\sigma,A)/h,
\$
where we recall the definition of $R_h$ and $L_h$ in~\eqref{eq:def_Rh_Lh}. 
Thus~\eqref{eq:obj_gap} follows from the bounds on $R_h$ and $L_h$ we obtained in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:balance}, e.g., equation~\eqref{eq:Rh_Lh_L2_bd}. 

Now choose $h_n = \sqrt{\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n}+\epsilon_n}$ which is smaller than $1/\log n$ for all sufficiently large $n$, and $\Gamma_n/(\sqrt{n}h_n)$ and $\epsilon_n/h_n$ are both upper bounded by $h_n$. 
Furthermore, we consider the event $\mathcal{E}_n$ on which there are sufficiently many controls in each cell defined in Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_local_cells}. On the event $\mathcal{E}_n$, each treated vertex can be matched to a distinct control with a Euclidean distance below $r_n$, which gives $Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,A}^\star,A) \leq C r_n^2$. On $\mathcal{E}_n^c$, we have $Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,A}^\star,A) \leq b_A d$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} gives for every fixed $b>0$, one can find  a sufficiently large constant ${c_{\textnormal{grid}}}$ so $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_n^c)\leq n^{-b}$ when $n$ is large enough. Combining the bounds and choosing $b$ appropriately gives $Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,A}^\star,A) = O_P(r_n^2)$. The eigenvalue condition on $A$ yields 
\$
(a_A/2) L_D(\hat\sigma) \leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A) \leq Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,A}^\star,A) + \Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A) = O_P(r_n^2 + \epsilon_n),
\$
so $L_D(\hat\sigma)=O_P(r_n^2+\epsilon_n)$. 

Finally, since $\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A)=o_P(1/n)$, there exists a deterministic sequence $b_n\to 0$ such that $\mathbb{P}(n\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A)>b_n)\to 0$. To see this, for each integer $k$ one can choose $n_k$ so $\mathbb{P}(n\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A)>1/k)<1/k$ for all $n\geq n_k$, and then choose $b_n = 1/k$ between successive $n_k$. 
Now set $\epsilon_n=b_n/n$, then $\Delta_D(\hat\sigma,A) = O_P(\epsilon_n)$ and $n\epsilon_n = o(1)$. Putting this back to~\eqref{eq:obj_gap_2}, when $d\leq 3$, we know $\sqrt{n}\Gamma_n \to 0$ and $n(\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n}+\epsilon_n) = o(1)+n\epsilon_n = o(1)$. This leads to $B_D( \hat\sigma, g) = o_P(n^{-1/2})$. Finally, $L_D(\hat\sigma)=O_P(r_n^2+\epsilon_n) = o_P(1)$ by definition.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:hat_A_imbalance}
    Suppose $||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}=O_P(a_n)$ for a  deterministic sequence $a_n\to0$, and $\xi_n=O_P(\eta_n)$ for a deterministic sequence $\eta_n\to0$. Then the optimization gap under the oracle balancing objective satisfies 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:metric-gap}
 Q_{D}(\hat\sigma,A_0)-\min_\sigma Q_{D}(\sigma,A_0)
 =O_P(a_n r_n^2+\eta_n).
\end{equation}
Whenever $(a_n r_n^2+\eta_n)(\log n)^2\to0$, equation~\eqref{eq:obj_gap_2} applies with $\epsilon_n=a_n r_n^2+\eta_n$. 
Moreover, if $||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}=O_P(n^{-1/2})$, then~\eqref{eq:balance-rate} holds under $\hat{A}$ for the corresponding exact minimizer, i.e., 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:balance-rate-hat-A}
 B_{D}(\hat\sigma, g)=O_P \big(  
 n^{-2/d}(\log n)^{1+2/d}
 +n^{-1/4-1/d}(\log n)^{1/2+1/d} \big),
\end{equation}
which obeys $\sqrt{n} B_{D}(\hat\sigma, g) =o_P(1)$ for $d\leq 3$. 
Finally, if $\xi_n = O_P(1/n)$, then $B_D(\hat\sigma, g)=o_P(n^{-1/2})$ when  $d\leq 3$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Work on the event that $\hat A$ has eigenvalues between $a_{A_0}/2$ and $2b_{A_0}$ and that $\mathcal{E}_n$ defined in Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} holds. Its probability tends to one. 
Since on $\mathcal{E}_n$ one can match all  treated  units to distinct control units in the same cell, the oracle optimizer $\sigma_{D,A_0}^\star$ has $L_D(\sigma_{D,A_0}^\star)\le Cr_n^2$, while $L_D(\hat\sigma)\le C(r_n^2+\xi_n)$ applying the eigenvalue condition on $\hat{A}$ on this event. 

For any injection $\sigma$, we know $|Q_{D}(\sigma,A_0)-Q_{D}(\sigma,\hat{A})|\le\frac12||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot L_D(\sigma)$. Thus 
\$
0 &\leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A_0) - \min_{\sigma} Q_D(\sigma, A_0) \\
&= \big\{ Q_D(\hat\sigma,A_0) - Q_D(\hat\sigma,\hat{A}) \big\}
+ \big\{ Q_D(\hat\sigma,\hat{A}) - Q_D(\sigma_{D,\hat{A}}^\star, \hat{A}) \big\}
+  \big\{ Q_D(\sigma_{D,A_0}^\star, \hat{A}) - Q_D(\sigma_{D,A_0}^\star,A_0) \big\}.
\$
Applying the above bounds for the first and third terms and the optimization gap bound for the second yield
\$
0 \leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A_0) - \min_{\sigma} Q_D(\sigma, A_0) &\leq \frac12||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot  \big\{ L_D(\hat\sigma) + L_D(\sigma_{D,A_0}^\star)\big\} + \xi_n \\ 
&\leq C ||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}\cdot r_n^2 + (1+ C||\hat A-A_0||_{\mathrm{op}}) \xi_n
\$
which proves \eqref{eq:metric-gap}. If $a_n=n^{-1/2}$ and $\eta_n=0$, 
then optimization gap becomes
\$
0 \leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A_0) - \min_{\sigma} Q_D(\sigma, A_0)  \leq O_P(r_n^2/\sqrt{n}) ,
\$ 
where 
$r_n^2/\sqrt n = o(\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n})$, so plugging into~\eqref{eq:obj_gap_2} with $\epsilon_n = \Gamma_n/\sqrt{n} = o((\log n)^{-2})$ yields 
\$
|B_{D}(\hat\sigma, g)| = O_P(\Gamma_n + (\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n} +\epsilon_n)^{1/2})
= O_P(\Gamma_n + (\Gamma_n/\sqrt{n})^{1/2}),
\$
and therefore~\eqref{eq:balance-rate-hat-A}  and the root-$n$ bound follow from the definition of $\Gamma_n$. 
Finally, if $\xi_n=o_P(1/n)$, then the optimization gap becomes 
\$
0 \leq Q_D(\hat\sigma,A_0) - \min_{\sigma} Q_D(\sigma, A_0)  \leq O_P(r_n^2/\sqrt{n}) + o_P(1/n) = o_P(1/n),
\$ 
since for $d\leq 3$  we know $r_n^2/\sqrt{n} = (\log n)^{2/d} n^{-1/2-2/d} = o_P(1/n)$. Thus by the last claim in Theorem~\ref{thm:obj_gap} we know 
$B_{D}(\hat\sigma,g)= o_P(1/\sqrt{n})$. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:cont_SDt}
Following the definitions above, one has 
    \@
|S_{D,t}(g) - S_{D,t}(f) |\leq 2\|g-f\|_{\infty},\quad g,f\in \mathcal{F}_L, ~t\neq 0.
\@
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif:replacement}
For every fixed $g,f\in\mathcal F_L$, deterministic $t\ne0$, and
$k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif:replacement}
 \left|\{S_{D,t}(g)-S_{D,t}(f)\}
       -\{S_{D^{(k)},t}(g)-S_{D^{(k)},t}(f)\}\right|
 \le\frac4{n_1}\|g-f\|_\infty
 \quad\text{almost surely}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif:quotients}
Suppose $d\le3$. Let $h_n\in(0,1/\log n]$ be a deterministic sequence with  $nh_n^2+\frac{\Gamma_n^2}{h_n^2}\to 0$. 
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif:secants-conclusion}
 \sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|S_{D,h_n}(g)|
 +\sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|S_{D,-h_n}(g)|
 \to 0\quad\text{in probability}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
We prove the result for $t_n:=h_n$ first. 
Since $d\le3$, plugging in the definition gives 
$n\Gamma_n^2\to0$. By \eqref{eq:bd_SDt}, for every fixed $g$, 
\[
 \mathbb E\{\mathbb Z_{n,t_n}(g)^2\}
 =n\operatorname{Var}\{S_{D,t_n}(g)\}
 \le C\left\{n(\Gamma_n+h_n)^2+
                       \frac{\Gamma_n^2}{h_n^2}\right\} \to 0. 
\]
Here $n(\Gamma_n+h_n)^2\le2n\Gamma_n^2+2nh_n^2$. 
Thus $\mathbb Z_{n,t_n}(g) = o_P(1)$ for any fixed $g$. 
By~\eqref{eq:bd_subgaussian}, Lemma~\ref{lem:unif:chain}
therefore gives
$\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|\mathbb Z_{n,t_n}(g)|=o_P(1)$. On the other hand, we have  
$\sqrt n\sup_g|\mathbb E[ S_{D,t_n}(g)]|
\le C\sqrt n(\Gamma_n+h_n)\to0$ by the same conditions by~\eqref{eq:bd_subgaussian}. Thus by the triangle inequality and by the definition of $\mathbb{Z}_{n,t}(g)$, 
\$
\sqrt n\sup_{g\in \mathcal{F}_L}|S_{D,t_n}(g)| \leq \sup_{g\in \mathcal{F}_L} \mathbb{Z}_{n,t_n}(g) +\sqrt n\sup_{g\in \mathcal{F}_L}|\mathbb E[ S_{D,t_n}(g)]| = o_P(1). 
\$
The result for $t_n:=-h_n$ follows in a similar way, which can be combined using a union bound.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Fix $L\in(0,\infty)$ and write $\pi=\mathbb P(Z=1)$.
We set all the optimization gaps and imbalance measures in this proof to zero on the event 
$\mathcal I_n^c=\{n_1<2\}\cup\{n_1>n_0\}$, as stated earlier. 


The propensity bounds imply
$\eta_0\le\pi\le1/2-\eta_1$.
Conditional on the treatment label vector, the
grouped covariates have distribution
$P_1^{\otimes n_1}\otimes P_0^{\otimes n_0}$, with densities
\[
p_1(x)=\frac{f(x)e(x)}{\pi},
\qquad
p_0(x)=\frac{f(x)\{1-e(x)\}}{1-\pi},
\]
where $n_1=\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i$ and $n_0=n-n_1$, and both are random counts. 
Let $\rho=(1/2+\eta_1)/(1/2-\eta_1)>1$. 
The density and derivative bounds required by Assumption~\ref{ass:cov} therefore follow from Assumption~\ref{assump:obs-sample}.

For $q=n_1/n$, we know 
\[
\inf_{x\in\Omega}\frac{(n-k)p_0(x)}{kp_1(x)}
\ge \frac{1-q}{q}\frac{\pi}{1-\pi}\rho.
\]
The right side is continuous in $q\in(0,1)$ and equals $\rho$
at $q=\pi$. Choose fixed $\delta>0$ and $\eta>0$ so that
$[\pi-\eta,\pi+\eta]\subseteq(0,1/2)$, $\eta<\pi$, and the last
display is at least $1+\delta$ whenever $|q-\pi|\le\eta$.
Define
\[
\mathcal K_n
=\{k\in\{0,\ldots,n\}:|k/n-\pi|\le\eta\},
\qquad
\mathcal J_n=\{n_1\in\mathcal K_n\}.
\]
For all sufficiently large $n$, we know counts in $\mathcal K_n$
satisfy $2\le k\le n-k$, as well as the lower bounds on
$k/n$ and $(n-k)/n$ and the margin $\delta$ required in Assumption~\ref{ass:cov}. 
Since $n_1\sim\operatorname{Bin}(n,\pi)$, 
Chernoff's inequality gives 
$\mathbb P(\mathcal J_n^c)\le2e^{-cn}$ for a fixed $c>0$.
In particular, $\mathbb P(\mathcal I_n)\to1$.


Write $a_0=\lambda_{\min}(A_0)>0$ and
$\delta_{A,n}=\|\hat A-A_0\|_{\mathrm{op}}$.
Use the same constants $r_n$, $\Gamma_n$ and $H_n(t)$ for all $k\in\mathcal K_n$. 
We consider the local-count event $\mathcal{E}_n$ defined in Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable}; 
following the same ideas there, for any fixed $b>0$, we can choose $c_{\text{grid}}$  so that
\[
\sup_{k\in\mathcal K_n}
\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n^c\mid n_1=k)\le n^{-b}
\]
for all sufficiently large $n$.
It follows by averaging that
$\mathbb P(\mathcal J_n\cap\mathcal E_n^c)\le n^{-b}$.
Also $\mathbb P(\delta_{A,n}\le a_0/2)\to1$.
Thus the event
$\mathcal J_n\cap\mathcal E_n\cap\{\delta_{A,n}\le a_0/2\}$
has probability tending to one.

On this event, the proofs of Theorem~\ref{thm:obj_gap} and Theorem~\ref{thm:hat_A_imbalance} give
\[
\begin{split}
\Delta_n
&:=Q_n(\hat\sigma_n;A_0)
       -\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}}Q_n(\sigma;A_0) \le C\delta_{A,n}r_n^2+(1+C\delta_{A,n})\xi_n.
\end{split}
\] 
All constants are common to $k\in\mathcal K_n$.
The assumed unconditional rates therefore imply
\[
\Delta_n
=O_P(n^{-1/2}r_n^2)+o_P(n^{-1})
=o_P(n^{-1}),
\]
since $\sqrt n\,r_n^2\asymp
n^{1/2-2/d}(\log n)^{2/d}\to0$ for $d\le3$.
Choose a deterministic $\epsilon_n>0$ such that
$n\epsilon_n\to0$ and
$\mathbb P(\Delta_n>\epsilon_n)\to0$.
Such a sequence exists because $n\Delta_n\to0$ in probability,
by the construction near the end of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:obj_gap}.


For $k\in\mathcal K_n$, let
$\mathbb P_{n,k}=P_1^{\otimes k}\otimes P_0^{\otimes(n-k)}$.
We then write 
$\Delta_D(\sigma,A_0)=Q_D(\sigma,A_0)-\min_\tau Q_D(\tau,A_0)$
and  
\[
R_{n,k}(D)
=
\max_{\substack{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{k,n-k}\\
                 \Delta_D(\sigma,A_0)\le\epsilon_n}}
\sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|B_D(\sigma,g)|.
\]
The maximum is over a nonempty finite set, and the inner supremum is measurable since it takes 
the supremum over a fixed countable dense subset of $\mathcal F_L$. 
Choose the first maximizing injection in a fixed ordering and denote it as $\bar\sigma_{n,k}(D)$. Since it maximizes over finitely many measurable objectives, we know $\bar\sigma_{n,k}(D)$ is measurable.  

For every deterministic sequence $k_n\in\mathcal K_n$, all the
fixed-size assumptions hold with common constants and
$n\epsilon_n\to0$. 
Thus, Theorem~\ref{thm:uniform_balance} 
applied to $\bar\sigma_{n,k_n}$ gives 
$R_{n,k_n}\to0$ in $\mathbb P_{n,k_n}$-probability.
Consequently, 
\[
\sup_{k\in\mathcal K_n}
\mathbb{P}_{n,k}\{R_{n,k}>u\}\to 0.
\]
for every $u>0$. 
To see this, if it failed, 
there would be a
subsequence of $(n,k_n)$ with 
$k_n\in \mathcal{K}_n$ for which $\mathbb{P}_{n,k_n}\{R_{n,k_n}>u\}\geq \delta$ for some constant $\delta>0$. 
This contradicts the stated result for the deterministic sequence $k_n$.

Now we return to the observational sample. 
On the event $\mathcal{J}_n\cap\{\Delta_n\le\epsilon_n\}$, the matching $\hat\sigma_n\in \mathcal{A}_{n_1,n_0}$ satisfies $\Delta_{D}(\sigma,A_0)\leq \epsilon$.  
Thus, for every $u>0$,
\[
\begin{split}
\mathbb{P} \Big\{\sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_L}|B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(g)|>u\Big\}
&\le \mathbb{P}(\mathcal{J}_n^c) +\mathbb{P}(\Delta_n>\epsilon_n) + \sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_n}\mathbb{P}(n_1=k) \mathbb{P}_{n,k}\{R_{n,k}>u\}\\
&\le \mathbb{P}(\mathcal{J}_n^c)
   +\mathbb{P}(\Delta_n>\epsilon_n)
   +\sup_{k\in\mathcal{K}_n}\mathbb{P}_{n,k}\{R_{n,k}>u\}
\to 0.
\end{split}
\]
This concludes the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:observational}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:observational-moments}
Under Assumption~\ref{ass:cov}, fix a symmetric
positive-definite matrix $A_0$ and a function $g\in\mathcal F_L$.
On $\mathcal I_n=\{2\le n_1\le n_0\}$, let $\sigma_n^\star$
be an exact minimizer of $Q_n(\sigma;A_0)$, with the fixed
measurable tie-breaking rule. Set
$B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)=0$ on $\mathcal I_n^c$.
Let $\Gamma_n=r_n^2\log n\asymp
n^{-2/d}(\log n)^{1+2/d}$ be the deterministic proof scale.
There is a constant $C_g<\infty$, independent of $n$, such that
for all sufficiently large $n$,
\[
|\mathbb E B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)|\le C_g\Gamma_n,
\qquad
\mathbb E[B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)^2]
\le C_g\left(\Gamma_n^2+\frac{\Gamma_n}{\sqrt n}\right).
\]
For $d\le3$, $\sqrt n B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)\to0$ in $L^2$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Use $\mathcal K_n$ and $\mathcal J_n$ from the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:observational}. 
Conditional on $n_1=k\in\mathcal K_n$, we obtain the deterministic two-sample law. 
By Theorem~\ref{thm:balance},  for every such $k$,
\[
\left|\mathbb E[B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)\mid n_1=k]\right|
\le C_g\Gamma_n,
\qquad
\mathbb E[B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)^2\mid n_1=k]
\le C_g\left(\Gamma_n^2+\frac{\Gamma_n}{\sqrt n}\right).
\]
for the common constant $C_g$. 
Otherwise for $k\notin \mathcal{K}_n$, we have
$|B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)|\le2\|g\|_\infty$.
Using
$\mathbb P(\mathcal J_n^c)\le2e^{-cn}$ gives
\[
\begin{split}
|\mathbb E B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)|
&\le C_g\Gamma_n+4\|g\|_\infty e^{-cn},\\
\mathbb E[B_{n,\sigma_n^\star}(g)^2]
&\le C_g\left(\Gamma_n^2+\frac{\Gamma_n}{\sqrt n}\right)
   +8\|g\|_\infty^2e^{-cn}.
\end{split}
\]
The remainders can be absorbed into the first terms for all sufficiently large $n$. Finally, by definition we have
$n\Gamma_n^2+\sqrt n\Gamma_n\to0$ for $d\le3$,
which proves the $L^2$ conclusion.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:control_set_stability}
    Let $c=(c_{ij})$ and $c'=(c_{ij}')$ be two cost matrices that differ by at most one row or one column. Suppose $\sigma_\star$ and $\sigma_\star'$ are the unique optimal matchings minimizing the corresponding matching costs. Then the selected control set either agree, $S_{\sigma_\star}= S_{\sigma_\star'}$, or they differ by deleting one element and adding another,  $S_{\sigma_\star'} = S_{\sigma_\star} \cup \{j'\} \backslash \{j\}$ for some $j'\neq j$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:count_stable}
    Fix $0<\zeta < \min\{\delta/8, 1/4\}$. For every $b>0$, ${c_{\textnormal{grid}}}$ can be chosen such that $\mathcal{E}_n:=\{ N_{Q,0}\geq (1+2\zeta) N_{Q,1} +4 \text{ for every }Q\}$ satisfies $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_n^c)\leq n^{-b}$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Moreover, suppose one-coordinate replacement applies to a dataset $D$ in $\mathcal{E}_n$ and yields the dataset $D'$, with the corresponding counts $N_{Q,1}'$ and $N_{Q,0}'$. Then the new counts satisfy $N_{Q,0}'\geq (1+\zeta) N_{Q,1}'$ for any cell $Q$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:local_edge}
Suppose $N_{Q,0}\ge(1+\zeta)N_{Q,1}$ in every cell. Then $|\mathcal N(S)|\ge(1+\zeta)|S|$ for every set $S$ of treated vertices. Given any partial matching with $n_1-1$ edges (not necessarily local), one can match the remaining unmatched treated vertex by adding and removing edges along a path of length at most $2\lceil\log n_1/\log(1+\zeta)\rceil+3$ and every added edge is a local edge defined above.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $\mathcal M_1,\mathcal M_2$ as the unique optimal matchings, so $M_1 = \{(i,\sigma_\star(i))\colon 1\leq i\leq n_1\}$ and $M_2 = \{(i,\sigma_\star'(i))\colon 1\leq i\leq n_1\}$. Let $v_*$ be the single treated or control vertex whose row or column in the cost matrix changes between $(c,c')$. Therefore, every edge with $v_*$ as an endpoint has the same cost in $c$ and $c'$.

Consider the symmetric difference between the graphs defined by $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$, i.e., the common edges are deleted after combining the edges in the two graphs. We denote this graph as $\mathcal{G}_0$. 
In this graph, a treated vertex $i$ has either degree of $0$ (if $\sigma_\star(i)=\sigma_\star'(i)$), or two edges (if $\sigma_\star(i)\neq \sigma_\star'(i)$ hence the edges from $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$ are both preserved. 
A control vertex $j$ has a degree of either $0$ (if it is matched to the same treated vertex in $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$), $1$ (if it is selected in one graph but not the other), or $2$ (if it is selected in both and matched to distinct treated vertices). 
Whenever the degree is $2$, the two edges must come from each of the matchings and connect to distinct vertices in the other group.  

We claim that in $\mathcal{G}_0$, each connected component is a path or a cycle. To see this, we consider two cases. 
\begin{itemize}
    \item First, if the component has any degree-$1$ vertex, we start a walk from any of such vertices following the edges. At any later, degree-two vertex, there is exactly one edge on which to continue without going straight back; for such vertex, this walk continues following that edge. This cannot branch since it would create a vertex of degree $3$. 
    Moreover, this walk cannot enter a cycle because otherwise this entry point would have a degree of three. Since there are finitely many vertices, this walk must end at another degree-one vertex, which traverses a sequence of distinct degree-two vertices and this component is a path. 

    \item On the other hand, if the component has no degree-one vertex, every vertex has degree two with distinct endpoints. Starting from any vertex must come back to it and create a cycle. No additional edge can attach to that cycle without creating a vertex of degree $3$. 
\end{itemize}

                                                                        

Next, we claim that, traversing  each of these paths and cycles, the edges must alternate between $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$. 
To see this, because $\mathcal{G}_0$ is the symmetric difference, every edge must appear only in exactly one of the two matchings. As we traverse in any path or cycle, the edges attached to a vertex of degree $2$ must come from different matchings; otherwise, if the two edges come from the same matching, it either connects to the same control vertex twice, which is impossible, or connects to distinct control vertices in the same matching, which is also impossible. 
In words, when traversing each path or cycle, the vertices alternate between treated and control groups, and the edges alternate between $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$. 

As we argued earlier, a treated vertex cannot have degree $1$ in $\mathcal{G}_0$, so every path must have two control endpoints. The number of edges in every path must be even, since edges run between the treated/control groups and both endpoints are controls. Thus, the first and last edges must belong to different matchings; one endpoint is in $S_{\sigma_\star}$ (selected in $\mathcal{M}_1$) and one endpoint is in $S_{\sigma_\star'}$ (selected in $\mathcal{M}_2$). 
On the other hand, on a cycle, every control vertex is in both $S_{\sigma_\star}$ and $S_{\sigma_\star'}$. 
 


                                                       
Recall that $v_\star$ is the single treated or control vertex whose row or column in the cost matrix changes. 

\begin{claim}\label{clm:contain_v*}
    Every nonempty component in the symmetric difference graph $\mathcal{G}_0$ must contain $v_*$. 
\end{claim}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{clm:contain_v*}]
    Suppose a non-empty component $H$ in  $\mathcal{G}_0$ does not contain $v_*$. 
We define a \texttt{switching} operation, where \texttt{switching} this component means removing its edges in $\mathcal{M}_1$ (roughly denoted by $H\cap \mathcal{M}_1$) and inserting its edges in $\mathcal{M}_2$ (denoted by $H\cap \mathcal{M}_2)$, leaving all other edges unchanged. 

Starting from any injection (represented by a bipartite graph), \texttt{switching} the component $H$ yields another injection, 
since each treated vertex or each internal control (i.e., control vertices with degree $2$) still has degree $1$ (one edge in $\mathcal{M}_1$ removed and one edge in $\mathcal{M}_2$ added); moreover, the endpoint controls if $H$ is a path either changes from being selected to not or vice versa. No vertex outside $H$ is affected. Therefore, \texttt{switching} from $\mathcal{M}_1$ or $\mathcal{M}_2$ gives a feasible injection.

Define the total cost change for switching from $\mathcal{M}_1$ (which leads to $\mathcal{M}_2$) by
\begin{equation*}
 s_H=\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal M_2\cap H}c_{ij}
       -\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal M_1\cap H}c_{ij}.
\end{equation*}
That is, evaluated under $(c_{ij})$, the cost of $\mathcal{M}_2$ increases from $\mathcal{M}_1$ by $s_H\geq 0$ since $\mathcal{M}_1$ is optimal under $(c_{ij})$.

Now consider switching from $\mathcal{M}_2$, which leads to $\mathcal{M}_1$. 
Since $v^*$ is not in $H$, every edge in $H$ has the same cost in $(c_{ij})$ and $(c_{ij}')$, so this switch evaluated under $c'$, changes its cost (from $\mathcal{M}_2$ to $\mathcal{M}_1$) by $-s_H$. Optimality of $\mathcal{M}_2$ under $c'$ gives $-s_H\ge0$. Hence $s_H=0$. But $H$ is non-empty and contains only non-common edges, so the switch from $\mathcal{M}_1$ to $\mathcal{M}_2$ produces a different matching with the same minimum cost under $c$. This contradicts the uniqueness.
  
Thus, every nonempty component must contain $v_*$. 
\end{proof}

By Claim~\ref{clm:contain_v*}, since different components in $\mathcal{G}_0$ have no common vertex, there is at most one non-empty component in $\mathcal{G}_0$. 
If none exists, the graph $\mathcal{G}_0$ is empty, and all edges in $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$ agree, so $S_{\sigma^\star} = S_{\sigma_\star'}$. 
If there is one component, and if it is a cycle, then because each internal control vertex in the cycle is selected in both $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$, we know $S_{\sigma^\star} = S_{\sigma_\star'}$.  
If it is a path, then every internal control vertex is selected in both $\mathcal{M}_1$ and $\mathcal{M}_2$. 
Only the two control endpoints are selected in only one of the two matchings; moreover, by our earlier arguments, they belong to different matchings, thus $S_{\sigma_\star'}$ is obtained by removing one endpoint control vertex $j$ from $S_{\sigma^\star}$ and add another $j'$. This proves Lemma~\ref{lem:control_set_stability}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
\label{clm:contain_v*}
    Every nonempty component in the symmetric difference graph $\mathcal{G}_0$ must contain $v_*$.
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Suppose a non-empty component $H$ in  $\mathcal{G}_0$ does not contain $v_*$. 
We define a \texttt{switching} operation, where \texttt{switching} this component means removing its edges in $\mathcal{M}_1$ (roughly denoted by $H\cap \mathcal{M}_1$) and inserting its edges in $\mathcal{M}_2$ (denoted by $H\cap \mathcal{M}_2)$, leaving all other edges unchanged. 

Starting from any injection (represented by a bipartite graph), \texttt{switching} the component $H$ yields another injection, 
since each treated vertex or each internal control (i.e., control vertices with degree $2$) still has degree $1$ (one edge in $\mathcal{M}_1$ removed and one edge in $\mathcal{M}_2$ added); moreover, the endpoint controls if $H$ is a path either changes from being selected to not or vice versa. No vertex outside $H$ is affected. Therefore, \texttt{switching} from $\mathcal{M}_1$ or $\mathcal{M}_2$ gives a feasible injection.

Define the total cost change for switching from $\mathcal{M}_1$ (which leads to $\mathcal{M}_2$) by
\begin{equation*}
 s_H=\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal M_2\cap H}c_{ij}
       -\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal M_1\cap H}c_{ij}.
\end{equation*}
That is, evaluated under $(c_{ij})$, the cost of $\mathcal{M}_2$ increases from $\mathcal{M}_1$ by $s_H\geq 0$ since $\mathcal{M}_1$ is optimal under $(c_{ij})$.

Now consider switching from $\mathcal{M}_2$, which leads to $\mathcal{M}_1$. 
Since $v^*$ is not in $H$, every edge in $H$ has the same cost in $(c_{ij})$ and $(c_{ij}')$, so this switch evaluated under $c'$, changes its cost (from $\mathcal{M}_2$ to $\mathcal{M}_1$) by $-s_H$. Optimality of $\mathcal{M}_2$ under $c'$ gives $-s_H\ge0$. Hence $s_H=0$. But $H$ is non-empty and contains only non-common edges, so the switch from $\mathcal{M}_1$ to $\mathcal{M}_2$ produces a different matching with the same minimum cost under $c$. This contradicts the uniqueness.
  
Thus, every nonempty component must contain $v_*$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $\beta=1+2\zeta$. Since $\zeta<\delta/8$, we have
$\beta<1+\delta$. Hence we may choose a fixed
$\epsilon\in(0,1)$ sufficiently small so that 
$\kappa:=(1-\epsilon)(1+\delta) -\beta(1+\epsilon)>0$. 

For a fixed cell $Q$, consider the event 
$\mathcal G_Q
=
\left\{
N_{Q,0}\ge(1-\epsilon)\mu_{Q,0},~
N_{Q,1}\le(1+\epsilon)\mu_{Q,1}
\right\}$. 
On $\mathcal G_Q$, Assumption~\ref{ass:cov} implies
\$
N_{Q,0}-\beta N_{Q,1} \ge
(1-\epsilon)\mu_{Q,0}
-\beta(1+\epsilon)\mu_{Q,1} \ge
\left\{(1-\epsilon)(1+\delta)
-\beta(1+\epsilon)\right\}\mu_{Q,1} =\kappa\mu_{Q,1}.
\$
By the construction of the partition and the fixed lower bounds
on $n_1/n$ and $p_1$, there exists a constant $c_0>0$ such that, 
$\inf_{Q}\mu_{Q,1}\ge c_0 c_{\mathrm{grid}}\log n$ 
for all sufficiently large $n$. Therefore, for sufficiently large
$n$, 
$\kappa\mu_{Q,1}\ge4$, 
and hence $\mathcal G_Q$ implies 
$N_{Q,0}\ge \beta N_{Q,1}+4$. 

It remains to bound the probability that $\mathcal G_Q$ fails.
Since $N_{Q,0}$ and $N_{Q,1}$ are binomial, Lemma~\ref{lem:chernoff} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb P(\mathcal G_Q^c)
&\le
\mathbb P\!\left\{
N_{Q,0}<(1-\epsilon)\mu_{Q,0}
\right\}
+
\mathbb P\!\left\{
N_{Q,1}>(1+\epsilon)\mu_{Q,1}
\right\}\\
&\le
e^{- \epsilon^2 \mu_{Q,0}/2} 
+
e^{- \epsilon^2\mu_{Q,1}/(2+\epsilon)} \le
2\exp\{-c_1c_{\mathrm{grid}}\log n\}
=
2n^{-c_1c_{\mathrm{grid}}},
\end{aligned}
\]
for a constant $c_1>0$ depending only on the fixed regularity
constants and on $\delta,\zeta$.
  
There are $q_n^d\le Cn/(c_{\mathrm{grid}}\log n)$ cells for all
sufficiently large $n$. A union bound therefore gives
\[
\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n^c)
\le
\frac{Cn}{c_{\mathrm{grid}}\log n}
\,n^{-c_1c_{\mathrm{grid}}}.
\]
For any fixed $b>0$, choosing $c_{\mathrm{grid}}$ sufficiently
large makes the right-hand side at most $n^{-b}$ for all sufficiently
large $n$.

We then verify the replacement claim. Suppose $D\in\mathcal E_n$.
If a treated observation is replaced, then for every cell $Q$, 
$N'_{Q,0}=N_{Q,0}$ and
$N'_{Q,1}\le N_{Q,1}+1$. 
Thus
\$
N'_{Q,0}-(1+\zeta)N'_{Q,1}
&\ge
(1+2\zeta)N_{Q,1}+4
-(1+\zeta)(N_{Q,1}+1) =
\zeta N_{Q,1}+3-\zeta>0.
\$
If instead a control observation is replaced, then 
$N'_{Q,1}=N_{Q,1}$ and 
$N'_{Q,0}\ge N_{Q,0}-1$, 
and hence
\$
N'_{Q,0}-(1+\zeta)N'_{Q,1}
&\ge
(1+2\zeta)N_{Q,1}+3
-(1+\zeta)N_{Q,1} = 
\zeta N_{Q,1}+3>0.
\$
Therefore 
$N'_{Q,0}\ge(1+\zeta)N'_{Q,1}$ 
for every cell $Q$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{Q}(S)$ be the cells containing at least one vertex in $S$. Every control in these cells belongs to $\mathcal N(S)$, so
\[
 |\mathcal N(S)|=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q(S)}N_{Q,0}
 \ge(1+\zeta)\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q(S)}N_{Q,1}
 \ge(1+\zeta)|S|.
\]

Now consider the stated partial mapping. 
We perform a search which starts at the unique unmatched treated vertex, forming a singleton treated vertex set $S_0$. 
Then, for each $k\geq 0$, we follow all \textit{local} edges from treated sets in $S_k$ to controls, and then follow all existing edges in the matching (not necessarily local) from controls to their treated partners. The new set $S_{k+1}$ is formed by adding the newly visited treated vertices to $S_k$. 
In this search, we ignore local edges already in the matching, because searching along them do not increase $S_k$.  
We claim that after a bounded number of rounds of search, ${\mathcal{N}}(S_k)$ must reach an unmatched control. 
To see this, if $\mathcal N(S_k)$ contains no unmatched control, then due to partial matching, every vertex in $\mathcal N(S_k)$ has a distinct treated partner, and all these partners are included in $S_{k+1}$. Thus $|S_{k+1}|\ge|\mathcal N(S_k)|\ge(1+\zeta)|S_k|$. This cannot persist for more than $\lceil\log n_1/\log(1+\zeta)\rceil+1$ rounds, because there are only $n_1$ treated vertices.


Whenever a new vertex $v$ is discovered for the first time, we record the \textit{first-discovery predecessor} $u=\text{pred}(v)$ as the other endpoint of the corresponding edge. For example,  $v$ may be a control vertex first reached because it is connected to some $u\in S_k$ by a local edge; or $v$ may be a treated vertex first reached because it is not in $S_k$ but is matched to some $u\in {\mathcal{N}}(S_k)$ hence first discovered in round $k+1$. 
Since each control vertex may be connected by multiple treated vertices with local edges, we fix a total ordering of the vertices and take $\text{pred}(v)$ as the first vertex in this ordering satisfying the \textit{first-discovery predecessor} criterion. 
In this way, every vertex $v$ has a unique predecessor. 

For the reached unmatched control vertex, we trace back the search recursively via first-discovery predecessors to the initial vertex. 
This path thus alternates between local edges not in the matching (since we ignore local edges in the matching) and edges in the matching, starting and ending with a local edge not in the partial matching. 
  
Now, we start from the partial matching, remove the matching edges on this path and add the other (local) edges. 
Thus, each internal vertex loses one edge and gains one, while both endpoints gain one. 
The resulting graph covers all treated vertices and uses every control at most once. Moreover, the added edges are local by construction. 
Since the search persists no more than $\lceil\log n_1/\log(1+\zeta)\rceil+1$ rounds and each round contributes at most $2$ edges to this path, the length of this path is at most $2\lceil\log n_1/\log(1+\zeta)\rceil+3$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Fix distinct injections $\sigma,\tau$ and an index $i$ with $\sigma(i)\ne\tau(i)$. Conditional on all controls and on all treated observations except $T_i$, the difference of their objectives is affine in $T_i$. The quadratic terms in $T_i$ and the two terms $tg(T_i)$ cancel. The linear coefficient is $n_1^{-1}A\{C_{\tau(i)}-C_{\sigma(i)}\}$, which is nonzero almost surely because the controls are independent with a density and $A$ is invertible. Equality requires $T_i$ to lie on a hyperplane, which has conditional probability zero. There are finitely many pairs of injections, so by the union bound, we know there are no ties almost surely.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
This proof uses Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} and Lemma~\ref{lem:local_edge}. 
We suggest reading Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_match_graph_setup} and Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_local_cells} before reading this proof.

Start from an optimizer (an injection, one-to-one matching which can be represented by a graph with $n_1$ edges as before) for $D$. We consider three cases for the one-coordinate replacement:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*).,leftmargin=2.5em]\setlength\itemsep{-0.25em}
    \item If a treated sample is replaced, remove its edge and retain the other $n_1-1$ edges with endpoints in $D'$. 
    \item If a selected control is replaced, remove its edge and retain the other $n_1-1$ edges with endpoints in $D'$. 
    \item If an unselected control is replaced, the original injection remains feasible with identical objective in $D'$.
\end{enumerate}

In the first two cases, the new counts bounds in $D'$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} applies, i.e., $N_{Q,0}'\geq (1+\zeta)N_{Q,1}'$ for any cell $Q$. 
Additionally applying Lemma~\ref{lem:local_edge} to $D'$, we know the partial matching obtained after the stated operations can be turned to a complete matching using $O(\log n)$ added and removed edges.
  
Every added edge costs at most $C(r_n^2+t^2)$ by~\eqref{eq:A2:edge-upper}. Every removed edge, including the initially deleted one evaluated in $D$, costs at least $-Ct^2$ by~\eqref{eq:A2:edge-lower}; deleting it can therefore increase the cost by at most $Ct^2$. All retained edges have the same endpoints and costs in the two datasets. The total increase (before normalization of $1/n_1$) is at most $C(r_n^2+t^2)\log n=CH_n(t)$. Division by $n_1\ge a_{\mathrm s}n$ and minimization in $D'$ give $V_{D'}(t)\le V_D(t)+CH_n(t)/n$.

For the other direction, we start from an optimizer in $D'$ and remove the affected edge if it is present. Performing the stated three cases yields a partial matching in $D$. Note that $D$  satisfies $N_{Q,0}\geq (1+\zeta) N_{Q,1}$ because $D\in\mathcal{E}_n$, hence Lemma~\ref{lem:local_edge} applies to $D$. The same edge bounds and normalization give $V_D(t)\le V_{D'}(t)+CH_n(t)/n$. 
Combining the two directions yields the desired result.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Fix $|t|\le1$. Since $\|x-y\|^2\le d$, every edge satisfies $|\ell_t(x,y)|\le b_A d/2+2\|g\|_\infty=:M_0$. 
    Thus $|V_{\bar D}(t)|\le M_0$ for every dataset $\bar D\in\Omega^n$. In particular, $|V_D(t)-V_{\bar{D}}(t)|\leq 2M_0$ for any $D,\bar D\in \Omega^n$. 
    Choose a fixed constant $b>2+4/d$ and use Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable} to 
    choose a fixed ${c_{\textnormal{grid}}}$ so that $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_n^c)\le n^{-b}$.

For notational simplicity, denote $(W_1,\dots,W_n)=(T_1,\dots,T_{n_1},C_1,\dots,C_{n_0})$, i.e., let  $W_k=T_k$ for $k\le n_1$ and $W_k=C_{k-n_1}$ for $n_1<k\le n$. 
For dataset $D=(W_k)_{k=1}^n$, for each $k$, let $D^{(k)}$ be the data obtained by replacing $W_k$ in $D$ by an independent copy from the corresponding treated or control covariate distribution. On the event $D\in\mathcal{E}_n$, Lemma~\ref{lem:stab_opt_value} gives $|V_D(t)-V_{D^{(k)}}(t)|\leq CH_n(t)/n$; on the event $D\notin \mathcal{E}_n$, the arguments early on gives $|V_D(t)-V_{D^{(k)}}(t)|\leq 2M_0$. Putting the two cases together we obtain 
\[
 \mathbb{E}\{V_D(t)-V_{D^{(k)}}(t)\}^2
 \le \frac{CH_n(t)^2}{n^2}+4M_0^2n^{-b}.
\]
Since $W_k$'s are independent and $V_D(t)$ is square-integrable, Lemma~\ref{lem:ES}, summed over $k\in[n]$, yields
\[
 \operatorname{{\rm Var}} V_D(t)\le\frac12\sum_{k=1}^n
       \mathbb{E}\{V_D(t)-V_{D^{(k)}}(t)\}^2
 \le\frac{CH_n(t)^2}{n}+2M_0^2n^{1-b},\quad \forall t\in [-1,1].
\] 
Indeed, $H_n(t)\ge\Gamma_n$ and $\Gamma_n^2/n\asymp n^{-1-4/d}(\log n)^{2+4/d}$, so $n^{1-b}/(\Gamma_n^2/n)=O\{n^{2+4/d-b}/(\log n)^{2+4/d}\}\to0$ by the choice of $b$. This proves the first bound in~\eqref{eq:value-bounds}.

We now proceed to prove the second bound in~\eqref{eq:value-bounds}. 
Let $\bar T_1\sim p_1$ be independent of the remaining observations. Define the coupled datasets
\[
 \bar D=(\bar T_1,T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1},C_1,\ldots,C_{n_0}),\qquad
 \bar D_x=(x,T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1},C_1,\ldots,C_{n_0}).
\]
Then $D$ and $\bar D$ has the same law, and 
\[
 \mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)=\mathbb{E}\{V_{\bar D_x}(t)-V_{\bar D}(t)\}.
\]
On the event $\bar D\in \mathcal{E}_n$, Lemma~\ref{lem:stab_opt_value} gives $|V_{\bar{D}_x}(t)-V_{\bar D}(t)|\leq CH_n(t)/n$ for every $x\in\Omega$. On its complement the bound is $2M_0$ for every $x\in \Omega$. Consequently,
\[
 \sup_{x\in\Omega}|\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)|
 \leq \mathbb{E}\Big\{ \frac{CH_n(t)}{n} \operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{\bar D\in \mathcal{E}_n\} +2M_0\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{\bar D\notin \mathcal{E}_n\} \Big\} 
 \leq \frac{CH_n(t)}{n}  + 2M_0 n^{-b}.
\]
The remainder is negligible because $H_n(t)\geq \Gamma_n$ and  
 $\frac{n^{-b}}{\Gamma_n/n}
 =O (\frac{n^{1+2/d-b}}{(\log n)^{1+2/d}} )\to 0$. 
Since $H_n(t)\ge\Gamma_n$, this proves the second bound in~\eqref{eq:value-bounds}. 

Finally, we prove the last claim. 
Write the measurable optimizer as $\sigma_t = s_t(D)$. 
On $\mathcal{E}_n$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable}, every cell $Q$ contains enough controls to match all the treated observations within that cell. Combining the cellwise injections gives a feasible $\bar\sigma$, since distinct cells have disjoint vertices. Each of its edges has length at most $r_n$, so~\eqref{eq:A2:edge-upper} and optimality give $V_D(t)\le C_1(r_n^2+t^2)$. On the other hand, averaging both sides of~\eqref{eq:A2:edge-lower} over any optimizer gives $V_D(t)\ge(a_A/4)L_D(\sigma_t)-C_2t^2$. Therefore $L_D(\sigma_t)\le C(r_n^2+t^2)$ on $D\in\mathcal{E}_n$.  
On the other hand, on the event $D\in \mathcal{E}_n^c$, the diameter of the cube gives $L_D(\sigma_t)\le d$. Combining the two cases, we obtain  $\mathbb{E} [L_D(\sigma_t)]\le C(r_n^2+t^2)+dn^{-b}$. Finally, noting that $n^{-b}/r_n^2=O\{n^{2/d-b}/(\log n)^{2/d}\}\to0$ yields the desired bound.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Fix a deterministic $t\in[-1,1]$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:unique},
the tilted objective has a unique minimizer at $t$ with
probability one. Fix a dataset in this event, and denote
its minimizer by $\sigma_t$.

For each injection $\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}$, write
\[
b_\sigma=B_{D,\sigma,A}(g),
\qquad
\varphi_\sigma(s)=Q_D(\sigma,A)+s b_\sigma.
\]
Here $D,A,g$ are held fixed, so $b_\sigma$ is a constant
and $\varphi_\sigma$ is affine in $s$. By definition,
$V_D(s)=\min_\sigma\varphi_\sigma(s)$. Recall $\sigma_t$ is the minimizer defined in~\eqref{eq:def_Vd_minimizer}.

If there is only one feasible injection, $V_D$ is affine
and the derivative formula follows immediately.
Otherwise, uniqueness and finiteness imply
\[
\gamma
:=
\min_{\sigma\ne\sigma_t}
\{\varphi_\sigma(t)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(t)\}>0.
\]
Set 
$M=\max_{\sigma\ne\sigma_t}|b_\sigma-b_{\sigma_t}|$ and  
$r=\frac{\gamma}{2(1+M)}>0$. 
For every $s$ with $|s-t|<r$ and every
$\sigma\ne\sigma_t$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\varphi_\sigma(s)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(s)
&=
\varphi_\sigma(t)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(t)
+(s-t)(b_\sigma-b_{\sigma_t}) \ge \gamma-|s-t|M
\ge \gamma-rM
\ge \gamma/2>0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus $\sigma_t$ remains the unique minimizer whenever
$|s-t|<r$, and in which case 
$V_D(s)-V_D(t)=(s-t)b_{\sigma_t}$. 
Taking the difference quotient shows that almost surely 
\@\label{eq:def_VD't}
V_D'(t)=b_{\sigma_t}=B_{D,\sigma_t,A}(g).
\@ 
Finally, 
$|B_{D,\sigma_t,A}(g)|
\le
\frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}
\bigl\{|g(T_i)|+|g(C_{\sigma_t(i)})|\bigr\}
\le 2\|g\|_\infty$. 
Therefore, the derivative $V_D'(t)$ can be well-defined to be a bounded measurable function because one can set it to be zero on the null event where
uniqueness fails.  
By Lemma~\ref{lem:Taylor}, each matched pair satisfies 
$g(T_i)-g(C_{\sigma_t(i)})=\nabla g(T_i)^\top\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}+R_i(t)$, 
where $|R_i(t)|\le(G_2/2)\|T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\|^2$. Averaging over $i$ therefore gives $\big|V_D'(t)-G_D(t) \big|\le(G_2/2)L_D(\sigma_t)$, where we define 
\[
 G_D(t)=\frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}
    \nabla g(T_i)^\top\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}, \quad \text{and}\quad   J_g=\mathbb{E}\{\nabla g(T_1)^\top A^{-1}\nabla g(T_1)\},
\]
and $J_g\geq 0$ is fixed constant to be used later. 
Therefore, by triangle inequality, 
\@\label{eq:bd_VD't_1}
|\mathbb{E} V_D'(t)|\leq |\mathbb{E} G_D(t) | + (G_2/2) \mathbb{E}[L_D(\sigma_t)] \leq |\mathbb{E} G_D(t) | + C(r_n^2+t^2).
\@ 
almost surely, where the second inequality uses the last claim in Lemma~\ref{lem:value-bounds}, and
Here $G_D(t)$ depends on $t$ through $\sigma_t$, whereas $J_g$ is a fixed nonnegative number.  
We shall further control the term $G_D(t)$ in~\eqref{eq:bd_VD't_1} together with $J_g$. 
The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:IBP} is in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_lem_IBP}. 
As preparation, define the functions
\@\label{eq:def_v_a}
v(x)=A^{-1}\nabla g(x), \quad a(x)=p_1(x)v(x).
\@
For the vector field $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_d)^\top$, its divergence is the scalar $\nabla\!\cdot a=\sum_{k=1}^d\partial_k a_k$. Both $a$ and its first derivatives are continuous and bounded. Let $\nu(x)$ denote the outward unit normal to
$\Omega=[0,1]^d$ at $x\in\partial\Omega$, and let $dS$
denote surface measure. On the relative interior of the face $x_k=1$, $\nu(x)=e_k$; on the face $x_k=0$,
$\nu(x)=-e_k$, where $e_k$ is the $k$th standard basis vector.
Intersections of faces have zero surface measure.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:IBP}
Recall the functions defined in~\eqref{eq:def_v_a}. 
For every deterministic $|t|\le1$,
\@\label{eq:IBP} 
 \mathbb{E} [G_D(t)]+tJ_g
 =n_1\bigg[&\int_{\partial\Omega}\{\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)\}
 a(x)^\top\nu(x){\,\mathrm{d}} S(x) -\int_\Omega\{\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)\}
 \{\nabla\!\cdot a(x)\}{\,\mathrm{d}} x\bigg].
\@
\end{lemma}


Now, write $G_2=\sup_\Omega\|\nabla^2g\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ and define the finite constant
\[
 K_a=\int_{\partial\Omega}|a^\top\nu|{\,\mathrm{d}} S
               +\int_\Omega|\nabla\!\cdot a|{\,\mathrm{d}} x.
\]
Indeed, $\sup_\Omega\|a\|\le\|p_1\|_\infty L_g/a_A$ and
$\nabla\!\cdot a=\nabla p_1^\top A^{-1}\nabla g+p_1\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}\nabla^2g)$. Thus
$\|\nabla\!\cdot a\|_\infty\le(\sup_\Omega\|\nabla p_1\|L_g+d\|p_1\|_\infty G_2)/a_A$.
The cube has volume one and $2d$ faces of area one, so $K_a$ is bounded independently of $n,t$. Also by the eigenvalue bounds we have $0\le J_g\le L_g^2/a_A$.

Taking absolute values in~\eqref{eq:IBP} and applying Lemma~\ref{lem:value-bounds} gives
\[
 \big|\mathbb{E} [G_D(t)] +tJ_g \big|
 \le n_1K_a\sup_{x\in\Omega}|\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)|
 \le CK_a\frac{n_1}{n}H_n(t)
 \le C'H_n(t).
\]
Hence we have 
\$ \big|\mathbb{E} [G_D(t)] \big|\leq C'H_n(t) + |t| J_g\leq  C'H_n(t)+|t|L_g^2/a_A .
\$
Combining this with~\eqref{eq:bd_VD't_1} yields $|\mathbb{E} V_D'(t)|\le C\{H_n(t)+|t|+r_n^2+t^2\}$. For large $n$, since $\log n\ge1$ we know $r_n^2+t^2\le H_n(t)$, which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:IBP}
Recall the functions defined in~\eqref{eq:def_v_a}. 
For every deterministic $|t|\le1$,
\@\label{eq:IBP} 
 \mathbb{E} [G_D(t)]+tJ_g
 =n_1\bigg[&\int_{\partial\Omega}\{\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)\}
 a(x)^\top\nu(x){\,\mathrm{d}} S(x) -\int_\Omega\{\mu_t(x)-\mathbb{E} V_D(t)\}
 \{\nabla\!\cdot a(x)\}{\,\mathrm{d}} x\bigg].
\@
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
For a dataset with a unique optimizer, continuity of the finitely many objectives keeps this optimizer unchanged in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the data. This allows us to obtain the differentiation of $V_D(t)$ along an entry $T_i$ in $D$. We prove the following after this proof.  

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:diff_VDt}
    It holds almost surely that 
    \@\label{eq:diff_VDt}
    \nabla_{T_i}V_D(t) = \frac1{n_1}
\left[A\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}+t\nabla g(T_i)\right].
    \@
\end{lemma}


Taking the inner product of~\eqref{eq:diff_VDt} with $v(T_i)=A^{-1}\nabla g(T_i)$, we have
\[
 \nabla_{T_i}V_D(t)^\top v(T_i)
 =\frac1{n_1}\nabla g(T_i)^\top\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}
    +\frac{t}{n_1}\nabla g(T_i)^\top A^{-1}\nabla g(T_i)
\]
almost surely. By Lemma~\ref{lem:diff_VDt}, equation~\eqref{eq:diff_VDt} holds almost surely under the joint distribution. 
By Fubini's theorem, equation~\eqref{eq:diff_VDt} holds for almost every $x$ for almost every fixed $D_{-i}$; we assign any bounded values for derivatives on null sets. Thus, summarizing over $i\in[n_1]$ and taking expectation yields 
\$
\mathbb{E}[G_D(t)+tJ_g] =  \sum_{i=1}^{n_1} \mathbb{E}\big[ \nabla_{T_i}V_D(t)^\top v(T_i) \big].
\$
We shall prove~\eqref{eq:IBP} by investigating each summation term. Throughout we fix $t\in [-1,1]$. 


Fix $i\in[n_1]$ and denote all observations other than $T_i$ as $D_{-i}$. We condition on $D_{-i}$. Let $F_i(x)=V_{D^{i\to x}}(t)$. 
Then by definition, $\nabla_{T_i}V_D(t) = \nabla F_i(x)$. 
Conditional on $D_{-i}$, the density of $T_i$ is still $p_1$, so for a.s.~$D_{-i}$,
\[
 \mathbb{E}[\nabla_{T_i}V_D(t)^\top v(T_i)\mid D_{-i}]
 =\int_\Omega\nabla F_i(x)^\top v(x)p_1(x){\,\mathrm{d}} x
 =\int_\Omega\nabla F_i(x)^\top a(x){\,\mathrm{d}} x.
\]
Marginalizing over $D_{-i}$ yields 
\$
\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{T_i}V_D(t)^\top v(T_i)] = \mathbb{E}\bigg[ \int_\Omega\nabla F_i(x)^\top a(x){\,\mathrm{d}} x\bigg].
\$

For fixed $D_{-i}$, every objective $Q_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)+t B_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)$  has the common $x$-dependent term $\{x^\top Ax/2+tg(x)\}/n_1$, plus an affine function of $x$. Thus $F_i(x) = \min_{\sigma}\{Q_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)+t B_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)\}$ is continuous and piecewise $C^1$ on every coordinate line, with finitely many breakpoints. Its Lipschitz constant is at most $(b_A\sqrt d+L_g)/n_1$ for $|t|\le1$ as the same bound holds for $Q_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)+t B_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)$. 
Moreover, by the arguments in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:value-bounds}, we know $|F_i(x)|\leq M_0:= b_Ad/2 + 2\|g\|_\infty$.  

Fix a spatial coordinate $k$ and write $x^{(k,u)}$ for a point whose $k$-th coordinate is $u$, with the other coordinates $x_{-k}$ held fixed. Apply the product rule to $F_i a_k$ on each of the finitely many smooth subinterval of $[0,1]$ and take the summation. Continuity cancels all internal endpoints, leaving
\[
\begin{split}
 &\int_0^1\partial_kF_i(x^{(k,u)})a_k(x^{(k,u)}){\,\mathrm{d}} u =F_i(x^{(k,1)})a_k(x^{(k,1)})
   -F_i(x^{(k,0)})a_k(x^{(k,0)})
   -\int_0^1F_i(x^{(k,u)})\partial_k a_k(x^{(k,u)}){\,\mathrm{d}} u.
\end{split}
\]
Now integrate over $x_{-k}\in[0,1]^{d-1}$ and sum over $k$. The endpoint terms are the face integrals with outward normals, so
\[
 \int_\Omega\nabla F_i^\top a{\,\mathrm{d}} x
 =\int_{\partial\Omega}F_i a^\top\nu{\,\mathrm{d}} S
     -\int_\Omega F_i\nabla\!\cdot a{\,\mathrm{d}} x.
\] 

                                                                                                                      
For every fixed $x$, we know $\mathbb{E}[F_i(x)]=\mu_t(x)$, since the remaining treated observations have the same joint distribution for every $i$, and relabeling treated indices does not change the minimum objective. Summing the preceding identities over $i$ therefore gives
\[
\mathbb{E}[G_D(t)+tJ_g] = \sum_{i=1}^{n_1} \mathbb{E}\big[ \nabla_{T_i}V_D(t)^\top v(T_i) \big] 
 =n_1\left\{\int_{\partial\Omega}\mu_t a^\top\nu{\,\mathrm{d}} S
                  -\int_\Omega\mu_t\nabla\!\cdot a{\,\mathrm{d}} x\right\}.
\] 

Apply the same coordinate calculation to the constant function $F_i\equiv1$ gives $\int_{\partial\Omega}a^\top\nu{\,\mathrm{d}} S=\int_\Omega\nabla\!\cdot a{\,\mathrm{d}} x$. 
Multiplying both sides by $\mathbb{E}[V_D(t)]$ and subtract from the above equation gives the desired result.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:diff_VDt}
    It holds almost surely that 
    \@\label{eq:diff_VDt}
    \nabla_{T_i}V_D(t) = \frac1{n_1}
\left[A\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}+t\nabla g(T_i)\right].
    \@
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Fix a deterministic $t\in[-1,1]$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:unique}
and absolute continuity of the covariate distributions,
the tilted optimizer is unique and all treated points lie in $\Omega^\circ$ (the interior of $\Omega$) with probability one. 
In the following we work on this event. 
  
Fix $i$, hold all other observations fixed, and define
\[
\varphi_\sigma(x)
=
Q_{D^{i\to x}}(\sigma,A)
+tB_{D^{i\to x},\sigma,A}(g),
\qquad \sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}.
\]
It's straightforward to see that $\varphi_\sigma$ is continuously differentiable in $x$,
and $V_{D^{i\to x}}(t)=\min_\sigma\varphi_\sigma(x)$ takes the minimum of finitely many such curves.
Let $\sigma_t$ denote the unique minimizer at $x=T_i$.

If $|\mathcal{A}_{n_1,n_0}|=1$, then $\phi_\sigma(x)$ is clearly differentiable in $x$. Otherwise, finiteness and uniqueness give
\[
\gamma
:=
\min_{\sigma\ne\sigma_t}
\{\varphi_\sigma(T_i)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(T_i)\}>0.
\]
By continuity of $\phi_\sigma(x)$ in $x$ and finiteness of $\sigma\in \mathcal{A}_{n_1,n_0}$, one can choose a constant $r_0>0$ with
$B_{r_0}(T_i)=\{x\colon \|x-T_i\|\leq r_0\}\subset\Omega^\circ$ such that 
$\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}}
|\varphi_\sigma(x)-\varphi_\sigma(T_i)|
<\gamma/3$ 
whenever  $\|x-T_i\|\leq r_0$. 
For every such $x$ and every $\sigma\ne\sigma_t$, we know  
$\varphi_\sigma(x)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(x)
>
\varphi_\sigma(T_i)-\varphi_{\sigma_t}(T_i)
- 2\gamma/3 \geq \gamma/3>0$.  
Consequently,
$V_{D^{i\to x}}(t)=\varphi_{\sigma_t}(x)$ whenever $x\in B_{r_0}(T_i)$.
Note that only the $i$th matched pair's cost depends on $x$, so differentiation
at $x=T_i$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A2:spatial-gradient}
\nabla_{T_i}V_D(t) := \frac{{\,\mathrm{d}} V_{D^{i\to x}}(t)}{{\,\mathrm{d}} x}\bigg|_{x=T_i}
=
\frac{{\,\mathrm{d}} \varphi_{\sigma_t}(x)}{{\,\mathrm{d}} x}\bigg|_{x=T_i} 
=
\frac1{n_1}
\left[A\{T_i-C_{\sigma_t(i)}\}+t\nabla g(T_i)\right]
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\end{equation}
This proves Lemma~\ref{lem:diff_VDt}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
By definition, $V_{D,g}(0)=V_{D,f}(0)$ for any $g,f\in \mathcal{F}_L$, thus $S_{D,t}(g) - S_{D,t}(f) = \frac{V_{D,g}(t)-V_{D,f}(t)}{t}$, where 
\$
\frac{V_{D,g}(t)-V_{D,f}(t)}{t} &\leq \frac{Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,f}(t),A_0) + t B_D(\sigma_{D,f}(t),g)- Q_{D}(\sigma_{D,f}(t),A_0) - t B_D(\sigma_{D,f}(t),f)}{t} \\ 
&= B_D(\sigma_{D,f}(t),g) - B_D(\sigma_{D,f}(t),f) \\
&= \frac{1}{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1} \big\{ g(T_i) - g(C_{\sigma_{D,f}(t)(i)}) - f(T_i) +f(C_{\sigma_{D,f}(t)(i)}) \big\} 
\leq 2\|g-f\|_\infty. 
\$
Applying the same idea to $\sigma_{D,g}(t)$ yields 
\$
\frac{V_{D,g}(t)-V_{D,f}(t)}{t}\geq B_D(\sigma_{D,g}(t),g) - B_D(\sigma_{D,g}(t),f)\geq  -2 \|g-f\|_\infty.
\$
Combining the two directions yields the desired result.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix $g,f\in\mathcal F_L$, $t\ne0$, and the replacement coordinate $k$.
Write $q=g-f$ and $g_u=f+uq$ for $u\in[0,1]$. Since $\mathcal F_L$ is convex, we know $g_u\in\mathcal F_L$. Let $\sigma_u$ and $\sigma'_u$ be the selected exact optimizers of
$V_{D,g_u}(t)$ and $V_{D^{(k)},g_u}(t)$, respectively.
For these fixed functions and $t$, the corresponding objectives $V_{D,g_u}(t)$ and $V_{D^{(k)},g_u}(t)$ 
are jointly measurable
in the data and $u$.  
 
Given the dataset $D$ and a fixed injection $\sigma$, the objective involved in $V_{D,g_u}(t)$ is affine in $u$:
\[ 
Q_D(\sigma,A_0)+tB_D(\sigma,g_u)
 =Q_D(\sigma,A_0)+tB_D(\sigma,f)+utB_D(\sigma,q).
\]
Thus $u\mapsto V_{D,g_u}(t)$ is the minimal of a finite family of affine functions.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope}, this map is absolutely continuous and at every differentiability
point its derivative is $tB_D(\sigma_u,q)$.
Integration over $u\in [0,1]$
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif:interpolation}
 S_{D,t}(g)-S_{D,t}(f)
 =\frac{V_{D,g}(t)-V_{D,f}(t)}t
 =\int_0^1 B_D(\sigma_u,q) {\,\mathrm{d}} u.
\end{equation}
The first equality used $V_{D,g}(0)=V_{D,f}(0)$ for any $g,f\in \mathcal{F}_L$. 
The same result holds for $D^{(k)}$ and $\sigma'_u$. 

For each fixed $u$, Lemma~\ref{lem:unique} applied to  $g=g_u$ gives
uniqueness of $\sigma_u$ and $\sigma_{u'}$ with probability one.
The event that either optimizer is non-unique is jointly measurable, so Tonelli's theorem
gives
\[
 \mathbb{E}\int_0^1
 \operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{\text{either optimizer at }u\text{ is non-unique}\}\,du=0.
\]
That is, for almost every coupled pair $(D,D^{(k)})$, both $(\sigma_u,\sigma_u')$
are unique for (Lebesgue)-almost every $u$. 

We now revisit the language in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_match_graph_setup}. For a fixed $u$, the cost matrix which defines the objective $Q_{D}(\sigma,A_0)+B_{D,A}(g)$ is given by 
$c_{ij}= \frac12(T_i-C_j)^\top A_0(T_i-C_j)
+t\{g_u(T_i)-g_u(C_j)\}$. 
Since the corresponding cost matrix $(c_{ij})$ and $(c_{ij}')$ for $D$ and $D'$ only differs from one row/column by replacing one treated/control data vector, Lemma~\ref{lem:control_set_stability} implies that the
selected control index sets $\mathcal S = \{\sigma_u(i)\colon i \in [n_1]\}$   and $\mathcal S'=\{\sigma_u'(i)\colon i\in [n_1]\}$ of $\sigma_u$
and $\sigma'_u$ either agree or differ by one deletion and one addition. 

We define the constant 
$\omega_q=\sup_\Omega q-\inf_\Omega q\le2\|g-f\|_\infty$. 
Write $D=(T_1,\dots,T_{n_1},C_1,\dots,C_{n_0})$ and $D^{(k)}=(T_1',\dots,T_{n_1}', C_1', \dots, C_{n_0}')$ for easier reference. 
Consider two cases of the replacement on $D$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $k\leq n_1$, i.e., a treated unit is replaced by a  copy, then $\big| \sum_{i=1}^{n_1} q(T_j') -  \sum_{i=1}^{n_1} q(T_j)\big|\leq \omega_q$. The control units remain the same, and due to the above arguments on ${\mathcal{S}}$ and ${\mathcal{S}}'$, we know $\big| \sum_{j\in {\mathcal{S}}'} q(C_j') - \sum_{j\in {\mathcal{S}}} q(C_j)\big|\leq  \omega_q$.  
    \item If $k>n_1$, i.e., a control unit is replaced, write $j_0 = k-n_1$ as its index. Then the treated summation is unchanged, while $ \sum_{j\in\mathcal S'}q(C'_j)-\sum_{j\in\mathcal S}q(C_j)
 =  \sum_{j\in\mathcal S'}q(C_j)
                   -\sum_{j\in\mathcal S}q(C_j) 
  +\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{j_0\in\mathcal S'\} \{q(C'_{j_0})-q(C_{j_0})\}.$
  The absolute value of the first term is at most $\omega_q$ since ${\mathcal{S}}$ and ${\mathcal{S}}'$ differs by at most one removal and one addition, and the absolute value of the second term is at most $\omega_q$. 
\end{itemize}
Therefore, in both cases, we have 
\[
 |B_D(\sigma_u,q)-B_{D^{(k)}}(\sigma'_u,q)|
 \leq \frac{2\omega_q}{n_1}
 \leq \frac4{n_1}\|g-f\|_\infty.
\]
This holds for almost every $u$ on the probability-one event we
constructed. Following~\eqref{eq:unif:interpolation}, 
we then know 
\$
\left|\{S_{D,t}(g)-S_{D,t}(f)\}
       -\{S_{D^{(k)},t}(g)-S_{D^{(k)},t}(f)\}\right|
= \bigg| \int_0^1 B_D(\sigma_u, q){\,\mathrm{d}} u 
- \int_0^1 B_{D^{(k)}}(\sigma_u, q){\,\mathrm{d}} u  \bigg| \leq \frac4{n_1}\|g-f\|_\infty.
\$
Note that the aforementioned probability-one event is needed for each fixed values of $g,f,t,k$ without requiring an intersection over the entire function
class.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:dimension_fixed}
For every fixed integer $d\ge4$, there are a density $p_0$ and a function $g$, both extending to $C^\infty$ functions near $\Omega=[0,1]^d$, and constants $\rho,c,C>0$, such that the following holds. The density $p_0$ is bounded above and away from zero, $0\le g\le1$, and $\|\nabla g\|_\infty\le1$. Let $p_1(x)\equiv 1$ for $x\in \Omega$, let the controls have density $p_0$, and let ${n_1}=\lfloor\rho {n_0}\rfloor$ for some fixed $\rho>0$, and ${n_0}p_0(x)\ge16{n_1}$ for all $x$. For all sufficiently large ${n_0}$, 
\@\label{sharp:eq:main-moments}
 c{n_0}^{-2/d}\le\mathbb{E} [B_{{n_1},{n_0}}(g)]\le C{n_0}^{-2/d},
 \qquad \operatorname{{\rm Var}}\{B_{{n_1},{n_0}}(g)\}\leq 2(n_1+n_0)/{n_1^2}.
\@
For $d=4$, there are fixed $c_0,p_*>0$ such that
\@\label{sharp:eq:four-prob}
 \liminf_{{n_0}\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\{\sqrt{{n_1}+{n_0}}\,B_{{n_1},{n_0}}(g)\ge c_0\}\ge p_*.
\@
For $d>4$, $\sqrt{{n_1}+{n_0}}\,B_{{n_1},{n_0}}(g)\to+\infty$ in probability.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{sharp:lem:IBP} 
For every $x\in\Omega$, it holds that  
$ J(x)\ge\frac12{\mathbb E}[\min_{1\le j\le n_0}\|x-C_j\|^2]\ge0$. 
If $2n_1\le n_0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:J-integral}
 \int_\Omega J(x)\,dx
 =\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1-1}
 \le\frac{\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}}{n_1}.
\end{equation}
Let $g(x)=q(x_1)$ for some function $q\colon [0,1]\to \mathbb{R}$ which has a $C^2$ extension near $[0,1]$ and
$q'(0)=q'(1)=0$. If $\sup_{x\in [0,1]} q''(x)\le M$  for some fixed $M\ge0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:bias-lower}
 \mathbb{E} [B_{n_1,n_0}(g)]
 \ge-\int_\Omega q''(x_1)J(x)\,dx
      -\frac{M}{n_1}\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}.
\end{equation}
Also, if $2n_1\le n_0$ and $H=\|q''\|_\infty$, then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:bias-upper}
 |\mathbb{E} [B_{n_1,n_0}(g)]|
 \le\frac{H}{n_1}
       \{\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}+\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{sharp:lem:two-estimates}
Let $p_0$ be given by \eqref{sharp:eq:p0}, with $a,w$ constructed as above.
There are constants $c_d,K_d>0$ depending only on $d$ such that, for all
sufficiently large $n_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:uniform-cost}
 \overline{\mathfrak C}_k\le K_d k n_0^{-2/d}
 \quad\text{for every integer }1\le k\le a n_0/4,
\end{equation}
and, for every $1\le n_1\le n_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:local-insertion-lower}
 \inf_{x\in\Omega,x_1\in J_w}J_{n_1,n_0}(x)
 \ge c_d a^{-2/d}n_0^{-2/d}.
\end{equation}
One may take $c_d=\tfrac14(2v_d)^{-2/d}$, where $v_d$ is the volume of the
unit Euclidean ball. The constants $c_d,K_d$ do not depend on $a,w$ or the
group sizes; the lower threshold on $n_0$ may depend on $a,w$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{sharp:prop:variance}
Let $1\le n_1\le n_0$. Suppose all $n_1+n_0$ covariate vectors are independent
and have Lebesgue densities on $\Omega$. Let $R_g=\sup_\Omega g-\inf_\Omega g$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:variance}
 \operatorname{{\rm Var}}\big\{B_{n_1,n_0}(g)\big\}\le  2R_g^2(n_1+n_0) / n_1^2.
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Conditional on a treatment-label vector, the covariates have law
$P_1^{\otimes n_1}\otimes P_0^{\otimes n_0}$. Define
\[
 \mathcal A_n=\mathcal I_n\cap\{\rho/2\le n_1/n_0\le2\rho\}.
\]
Then we know $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal A_n)\to1$. On this event,
$2n_1/n_0\le4\rho=a/4$ and $n_1\ge\rho n_0/2$. The bounds in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_dimension_construct} therefore hold with the same constants for all
these counts. In particular, these results state that
\[
 {\mathbb E}\{B_n(g)\mid n_1,n_0\}\ge\beta n_0^{-2/d},\qquad
 \operatorname{{\rm Var}}\{B_n(g)\mid n_1,n_0\}\le C_v/n_0,
 \qquad C_v=4/\rho+8/\rho^2.
\]
Applying Lemma~\ref{lem:expect_var_bd} conditionally gives
\[
 \mathbb{P}\{B_n(g)\ge(\beta/2)n_0^{-2/d}\mid n_1,n_0\}
 \ge\frac{\beta^2}{4C_v n_0^{4/d-1}+\beta^2}
\]
uniformly on $\mathcal A_n$ for all sufficiently large $n$.
If $d=4$, its right side is $p_*>0$, and its event implies
$\sqrt{n_1}B_n(g)\ge(\beta/2)\sqrt{\rho/2}=:b_*>0$.
Averaging over $\mathcal A_n$ proves \eqref{sharp:eq:iid-prob}.
If $d>4$, the conditional lower bound tends to one uniformly on
$\mathcal A_n$, because $n_0\ge n/(1+2\rho)$ there. On the corresponding event,
\[
 \sqrt n B_n(g)\ge(\beta/2)\sqrt n\,n_0^{-2/d}
 \ge(\beta/2)n^{1/2-2/d}\to\infty.
\]
Averaging again proves divergence in probability.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We use the graph language in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_match_graph_setup} to state the proof. 
For any values of $(T_2,\dots,T_{n_1})$ and $C$, matching the treated point at $x$ pays
at least $\tfrac12\min_j\|x-C_j\|^2$ in the full matching objective. 
Removing this edge $(x,C_j)$ yields an injection that matches the remaining $n_1-1$ treated points to the full
control list. The cost of those remaining edges is at least
$\mathfrak C_{n_1-1}$. 
Subtracting this term from both sides and taking the expectation therefore gives
$J(x)\ge\tfrac12{\mathbb E}\min_j\|x-C_j\|^2\ge0$.

By definition, integrating $x$ under the uniform distributed treated samples gives
\[
 \int_\Omega J(x)\,dx
 =\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1-1}.
\]
Next, we prove the upper bound on the right-hand  side. The proof of the following lemma is in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_proof_lem_supermod}. 


\begin{lemma}\label{sharp:lem:supermod}
For a finite set $S$ of such vertices, let
$\mathfrak C_C(S)$ be the minimum total cost of matching every unit in $S$
to a distinct member of $C$.
Let $S\subseteq S'$ be finite sets of labeled treated vertices, let $x$ be an
additional treated unit not in $S'$, and suppose $|S'|+1\le n_0$. Then for the same control list $C$,
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:supermod}
 \mathfrak C_C(S\cup\{x\})-\mathfrak C_C(S)
 \le
 \mathfrak C_C(S'\cup\{x\})-\mathfrak C_C(S').
\end{equation} 
\end{lemma}


                                                                            
                                         
Apply Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:supermod} with 
\[
 S=\{T_1,\ldots,T_k\},\quad
 S'=S\cup\{T_{k+1}\},\quad x=T_{k+2}.
\]
The expectation of the left increment in~\eqref{sharp:eq:supermod} is
$\overline{\mathfrak C}_{k+1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_k$, and the expectation
of the right increment in~\eqref{sharp:eq:supermod} is
$\overline{\mathfrak C}_{k+2}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{k+1}$.
Thus successive expected increments are nondecreasing. 
Furthermore,  $\mathfrak C_C(S)$ cannot decrease when a treated point is added since the cost of matching a treated-control pair is nonnegative. 
If $2n_1\le n_0$, every increment from
$\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}$ through $\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}$ is at
least $\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1-1}$. Hence
\[
 n_1\{\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1-1}\}
 \le\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}
 \le\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1},
\]
or equivalently
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:increment-average}
 0\le\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}-\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1-1}
 \le\frac{\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}}{n_1}.
\end{equation}



Fix the controls and all treated points except the inserted $x$. If $x$ is
matched to control $j$, it is straightforward to see that the other treated points must be optimally matched to
$C\setminus\{C_j\}$. The total optimum as a function of $x$ is therefore
\[
 F(x)=\min_{1\le j\le n_0} F_j(x),\quad 
 \text{where} \quad F_j(x) :=  \frac12\|x-C_j\|^2+
 \mathfrak C_{C\setminus\{C_j\}}(\{T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1}\}) .
\]
The second term in $F_j(x)$ is constant in $x$ for each fixed $j$. 
All the $F_j(x)$ functions have the common 
quadratic part $\|x\|^2/2$, and their pairwise differences $F_j(x)-F_{j'}(x)$ are affine in $x$. 
Whenever $C_j\neq C_{j'}$, 
the affine difference $F_j(x)-F_{j'}(x)$ is non-constant. Since controls have a
density, the $C_j$'s are distinct almost surely. Thus, except for a finite union of
hyperplanes, the minimizing index $j(x)$ of $F_j(x)$ is unique and
$\partial_1F(x)=x_1-C_{j(x),1}$.
Moreover function $F$ is Lipschitz, since the gradient of every $F_j(x)$ has a norm of at most
$\sqrt d$ on $\Omega$.
It is continuously differentiable on finitely many
intervals and continuous at their endpoints.

For fixed $x_{-1}=(x_2,\ldots,x_d)$, we integrate over $q'(x_1)\partial_1F(x)$ by parts
on these subintervals. By continuity, the interior endpoints are cancelled, and
$q'(0)=q'(1)=0$ cancels the two exterior terms. This gives
\[
 \int_0^1q'(s)\partial_1F(s,x_{-1}){\,\mathrm{d}} s
 =-\int_0^1q''(s)F(s,x_{-1}){\,\mathrm{d}} s.
\]
Since $T_{11}$ (the first coordinate of the sample $T_1$) follows the uniform distribution, the left-hand side is
\$
\int_0^1q'(s)\partial_1F(s,x_{-1}){\,\mathrm{d}} s = \mathbb{E}\Big[ q'(T_{11}) \cdot\big\{ T_{11} - C_{\sigma_n^\star(1),1} \big\} \,\big{|}\, T_{-1}=x_{-1},T_{2:n_1},C\Big].
\$
