Since $\int_0^1q''(x){\,\mathrm{d}} x=0$,  we can subtract  the constant
$\mathfrak C_{n_1-1}(T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1};C)$ on the right-handed side, hence 
\$
 \mathbb{E}\Big[ q'(T_{11}) \cdot\big\{ T_{11} - C_{\sigma_n^\star(1),1} \big\} \,\big{|}\, T_{-1}=x_{-1},T_{2:n_1},C\Big] = -\int_0^1q''(s) \big\{ F(s,x_{-1})- \mathfrak C_{n_1-1}(T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1};C) \big\} {\,\mathrm{d}} s .
\$
Integrating over $x_{-1}$ (each with  uniform distribution on $[0,1]$) gives (Fubini's Theorem applies for these bounded terms)
\$
 \mathbb{E}\Big[ q'(T_{11}) \cdot\big\{ T_{11} - C_{\sigma_n^\star(1),1} \big\} \,\big{|}\, T_2,\dots,T_{n_1},C\Big] = -\int_\Omega q''(x_1)\big\{ F(s,x_{-1})- \mathfrak C_{n_1-1}(T_2,\ldots,T_{n_1};C) \big\}{\,\mathrm{d}} x .
\$
Since the treated samples are exchangeable, the same calculation applies for each of the remaining observations. Averaging over these observations, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:gradient-identity}
 {\mathbb E}\left[\frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}
 q'(T_{i1})(T_{i1}-C_{\sigma_n^\star(i),1})\right]
 =-\int_\Omega q''(x_1)J(x)\,dx.
\end{equation}


For $s,t\in[0,1]$, Taylor expansion and $q''\le M$ give
\[
 q(t)\le q(s)+q'(s)(t-s)+\frac M2(t-s)^2.
\]
Applying this $s=T_{i1}$ and $t=C_{\sigma_n^\star(i),1}$ and averaging gives 
\[
 \mathbb{E} [B_{n_1,n_0}(g)]
 \ge-\int_\Omega q''(x_1)J(x)\,dx
 -\frac{M}{2n_1}{\mathbb E}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}
                  \|T_i-C_{\sigma_n^\star(i)}\|^2.
\]
The last sum has expectation $2\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}$, giving
\eqref{sharp:eq:bias-lower}. For the absolute bound, use the absolute Taylor
remainder with $H=\|q''\|_\infty$. The absolute gradient contribution is at
most $H\int J\le H\overline{\mathfrak C}_{2n_1}/n_1$, and the remainder is at
most $H\overline{\mathfrak C}_{n_1}/n_1$. This proves
\eqref{sharp:eq:bias-upper}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{sharp:lem:supermod}
For a finite set $S$ of such vertices, let
$\mathfrak C_C(S)$ be the minimum total cost of matching every unit in $S$
to a distinct member of $C$.
Let $S\subseteq S'$ be finite sets of labeled treated vertices, let $x$ be an
additional treated unit not in $S'$, and suppose $|S'|+1\le n_0$. Then for the same control list $C$,
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:supermod}
 \mathfrak C_C(S\cup\{x\})-\mathfrak C_C(S)
 \le
 \mathfrak C_C(S'\cup\{x\})-\mathfrak C_C(S').
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Take an optimal matching $M_1$ for treated units in $S$ and an optimal matching $M_2$ for treated samples in 
$S'\cup\{x\}$. 
We use the graph language in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_match_graph_setup}, viewing each as a graph with edges between matched pairs. 
We color the edges from the two matching in distinct colors, where two copies of any common edge are preserved. 
Starting at $x$, we follow its $M_2$ edge to reach a control. If this
control is unmatched in $M_1$, then stop; otherwise, we follow its $M_1$ edge to a treated
vertex in $S$, and then that vertex's edge in $M_2$. We continue this process until stopping at a control vertex that is unmatched in $M_1$. 
Every treated vertex reached after $x$ belongs to $S$ and has edges in both $M_1$ and $M_2$.
This component must be a path, because along the way the vertices reached must alternate between a control matched in $M_2$ and a treated vertex matched to the preceding control in $M_1$, and finally stop at a control unit matched in $M_2$ but not in $M_1$. This path cannot be attached to a cycle because every internal vertex must have a degree of $1$ and the vertex $x$ has a degree of $1$, while attaching a cycle would create a vertex with degree at least $3$. 

By construction, edges along this path alternates between $M_1$ and $M_2$. We now exchange colors along the path. The first matching now covers treated vertices in $S\cup\{x\}$, 
and the second covers treated vertices in $S'$. Each internal vertex loses one edge of each relevant
color and gains one, so each matching of the corresponding colors remain injections. Their combined cost
is unchanged. Minimizing separately over the two new treated sets gives
\[
 \mathfrak C_C(S\cup\{x\})+\mathfrak C_C(S')
 \le\mathfrak C_C(S)+\mathfrak C_C(S'\cup\{x\}),
\]
which is equivalent to \eqref{sharp:eq:supermod}. This argument holds even if there are ties.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Partition $\Omega$ into adjacent cubes of side length $w=2^{-j}$, where we use half-open cubes  except on the outer faces to obtain a disjoint partition. Let $\mathcal{Q}_w$ be the collection of these cubes, and let $Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w$. 
Suppose $p_0\ge a_Q$ on $Q$ for some fixed numbers $a_Q\ge a>0$.
Let $k$ be the number of treated points which obeys $1\le k\le a n_0/4$. 
For any cube $Q$, we call $H$ a subcube of $Q$ if there exists some $0<h\leq 2$ and $y\in \mathbb{R}^d$ such that $H=Q(y,h)$ and $z_\ell\leq y_\ell\leq z_\ell +w-h$ for $\ell=1,\dots,d$, where we define $Q(y,h)=\prod_{\ell=1}^d I(y_\ell,h)$, and $I(s,h)=[s,s+h)$ if $s+h<1$ and $[s,1]$ if $s+h=1$. 

Define the constant 
\[
 C_d=2d+4d\sum_{j=1}^\infty4^j\exp\{-2^{d(j-1)}/4\}<+\infty. 
\]


We will use the following lemma, whose proof is in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_dimension_lem_cost}. 
\begin{lemma}\label{sharp:lem:cost} 
For all sufficiently large $n_0$, it holds  uniformly over $1\le k\le a n_0/4$ that 
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:cost-bound}
 \overline{\mathfrak C}_k
 \le C_d k n_0^{-2/d}\sum_{Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w}|Q|a_Q^{-2/d}
       +d k^2\exp(-n_0a w^d/4).
\end{equation}  
\end{lemma}

We first prove~\eqref{sharp:eq:uniform-cost} by applying Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:cost}. 
Recall that
$I=[1/2,1/2+w]$ and $w=2^{-j}$. Both endpoints of $I$
are grid points of the partition $\mathcal Q_w$.
We may therefore take $a_Q=a$ for the cells whose
first-coordinate intervals lie between these endpoints,
and $a_Q=b$ for all other cells. The two groups of cells
have total volumes $w$ and $1-w$, respectively. Hence
\$
\textstyle{\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q_w}}|Q|a_Q^{-2/d}
=
w a^{-2/d}+(1-w)b^{-2/d}
\le 2,
\$
where we used $w\le a^{2/d}$ (by construction) and $b\ge1$.
Substituting this into~\eqref{sharp:eq:cost-bound} gives
\[
\overline{\mathfrak C}_k
\le
2C_d k n_0^{-2/d}
+
d k^2\exp(-n_0aw^d/4).
\]
 
We next bound the second term. Since $k\le n_0$, we have 
\[
\frac{d k^2\exp(-n_0aw^d/4)}{k n_0^{-2/d}}
=
d k n_0^{2/d}\exp(-n_0aw^d/4)
\le
d n_0^{1+2/d}\exp(-n_0aw^d/4) \leq 1
\]
for all sufficiently large $n_0$ and $1\le k\le an_0/4$. Consequently, we have
\[
\overline{\mathfrak C}_k
\le (2C_d+1)k n_0^{-2/d}.
\]
This proves~\eqref{sharp:eq:uniform-cost} with $K_d=2C_d+1$.
Note that the constant $K_d$ depends only on $d$.


We now prove~\eqref{sharp:eq:local-insertion-lower}. Fix $x\in\Omega$ with $x_1\in J_w$.
Let $v_d$ denote the volume of the unit Euclidean ball
in $\mathbb R^d$, and set 
$s_{n_0}=(2v_d n_0a)^{-1/d}$. 
Since $a,w$ are fixed, we have $s_{n_0}\le w/8$ for all
sufficiently large $n_0$.

Recall that the distance between every point of $J_w$ and either endpoint of $I_{\mathrm{flat}}$ is at least $w/8$.
If $y\in\Omega$ satisfies $\|y-x\|<s_{n_0}$, then we must have 
\[
|y_1-x_1|\le\|y-x\|<s_{n_0}\le w/8.
\]
Thus $y_1\in I_{\mathrm{flat}}$, and so $p_0(y)=a$.
It follows that, for each control observation $C_j$,
\$
\mathbb P\{\|C_j-x\|<s_{n_0}\}
&=
\int_{\{y\in\Omega:\,\|y-x\|<s_{n_0}\}}p_0(y)\,dy \le a v_d s_{n_0}^d
=\frac{1}{2n_0}.
\$ 
The probability that at least one control lies within
distance $s_{n_0}$ of $x$ is therefore at most
\[
\mathbb P\left\{\min_{1\le j\le n_0}\|x-C_j\|<s_{n_0}\right\}
\le
\sum_{j=1}^{n_0}
\mathbb P\{\|x-C_j\|<s_{n_0}\}
\le\frac12.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\mathbb P\left\{\min_{1\le j\le n_0}\|x-C_j\|\ge s_{n_0}\right\}
\ge\frac12.
\]
On this event, the squared distance from $x$ to its nearest
control is at least $s_{n_0}^2$. Hence Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:IBP} gives
\$
J(x) \ge
\frac12\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\le j\le n_0}\|x-C_j\|^2 \Big]\geq 
\frac{s_{n_0}^2}{2}
\mathbb P\left\{
\min_{1\le j\le n_0}\|x-C_j\|\ge s_{n_0}
\right\} \ge \frac{s_{n_0}^2}{4}
=
\frac14(2v_d)^{-2/d}a^{-2/d}n_0^{-2/d}.
\$
The required condition $s_{n_0}\le w/8$ does not depend
on $x$ or $n_1$. Thus the same bound holds for every
$x\in\Omega$ with $x_1\in J_w$ and every
$1\le n_1\le n_0$, which proves~\eqref{sharp:eq:local-insertion-lower}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{sharp:lem:cost} 
For all sufficiently large $n_0$, it holds  uniformly over $1\le k\le a n_0/4$ that 
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:cost-bound}
 \overline{\mathfrak C}_k
 \le C_d k n_0^{-2/d}\sum_{Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w}|Q|a_Q^{-2/d}
       +d k^2\exp(-n_0a w^d/4).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We continue with the language in the proof of Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:two-estimates}. In each cube $Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w$, choose an integer $J_Q\ge0$ and a finest side length
$h_0=w2^{-J_Q}$ satisfying
$1\le n_0a_Qh_0^d<2^d$. Such a choice exists for all sufficiently large $n_0$.
In particular, $h_0^2\le4(n_0a_Q)^{-2/d}$. 
We denote the set of these finest cubes as $\mathcal{Q}_{w,h_0}$. Merging every collection of $2^d$ cubes in $\mathcal{Q}_{w,h_0}$ produces a collection of cubes of side length $2h_0$, which we denote as $\mathcal{Q}_{w,2h_0}$. We can continue this process to produce the sets of cubes $\mathcal{Q}_{w,2^j h_0}$ for $j=1,\dots,J_Q$, each of which is obtained by merging $2^d$ adjacent cubes in $\mathcal{Q}_{w,2^{j-1} h_0}$. 

We construct a matching as follows. 
First, we match as many points as possible within each finest cube $H\in \mathcal{Q}_{w,h_0}$. 
Then, we merge the $2^d$ ``child'' cubes as described before, and within each $H\in \mathcal{Q}_{w,2h_0}$, we match as many of their remaining treated and
control points as possible. 
We continue this process to finally produce the cube $Q$ of side length $w$. 
In this process, after producing and matching within any cell $H$, one can see that there are exactly $(N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+$ treated points remain.  

We now bound the total cost $\|T_i-C_j\|^2/2$ for the matched pairs $(T_i,C_j)$ in the above process. 
First, all pairs matched within $H\in \mathcal{Q}_{w,h_0}$ satisfy  $\|T_i-C_j\|^2/2\leq dh_0^2/2$, 
and there are at most as many such
edges in $Q$ as the number of treated points in $Q$, whose expectation is $k|Q|$. Their expected
total cost is thus at most $2d k|Q|(n_0a_Q)^{-2/d}$.
Similarly, for pairs matched after producing $\mathcal{Q}_{w,2^j h_0}$ at each level $j\ge1$, the side length is $h_j=2^jh_0$; 
moreover, every new pair uses a treated
point left at the preceding level, so the number of new pairs are upper bounded by 
\$
\textstyle{\sum_{H\in \mathcal{Q}_{w,2^{j-1}h_0}}} ( N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+,
\$
where $N_{H,1}$ and $N_{H,0}$ are the number of treated and control samples in $H$, respectively. 
The following lemma bounds the expectation of this number. 


\begin{lemma}\label{sharp:lem:deficit}
For a cube $H\subseteq Q$ of volume $v=|H|=h^d$, let $N_{H,1}$ and $N_{H,0}$ be the
treated and control counts in $H$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:deficit}
 \mathbb{E}\big[(N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+\big]
 \le2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4),
 \quad \mathbb{P}(N_{H,1}>N_{H,0})\leq 2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4).
\end{equation} 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:deficit}]
Write $M_H=N_{H,1}$ and $K_H=N_{H,0}$. 
We know they are independent of each other, and $M_H\sim \text{Binom}(k,v)$, $K_H\sim \text{Binom}(n_0,p_H)$ for the marginal probability $p_H\ge a_Qv$.
For $t>0$, we know $(M_H-K_H)_+\le M_H e^{t(M_H-K_H)}$, so by independence and the
generating function of the Binomial distribution,  
\$
 \mathbb{E}\big[(M_H-K_H)_+\big] \le kv e^t(1-v+ve^t)^{k-1}
           (1-p_H+p_He^{-t})^{n_0}.
\$
Since $1+u\le e^u$, taking  $t=\log2$, the right-handed side is upper bounded by 
\[
2kv(1+v)^{k-1}(1-p_H/2)^{n_0} \leq  2kv\exp(kv-n_0p_H/2)
 \le2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4),
\]
where the second inequality uses the facts that $v\leq 1$ and $k\le n_0a/4\le n_0a_Q/4$. 
Finally, we know $\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{M_H>K_H\} \leq (N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+$, which gives the second inequality.  
\end{proof}



Therefore, we know the expected number of these pairs satisfies
\[
\textstyle{\sum_{H\in \mathcal{Q}_{w,2^{j-1}h_0}}} \mathbb{E}\big[( N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+\big] \leq  2k|Q|\exp\{-n_0a_Q h_0^d2^{d(j-1)}/4\}
 \le2k|Q|\exp\{-2^{d(j-1)}/4\}.
\]
These pairs incur the cost $\|T_i-C_{\sigma(i)}\|^2/2$ of at most $dh_0^22^{2j}/2$, the diameter of cubes in $\mathcal{Q}_{w,2^j h_0}$ at level $j$. 
Thus, the expected total cost incurred by new pairs matched at level $j$ is at most
\[
2k|Q|\exp\{-2^{d(j-1)}/4\} \cdot 4d(n_0a_Q)^{-2/d} 4^j/2 \leq 
 4d k|Q|(n_0a_Q)^{-2/d}
       4^j\exp\{-2^{d(j-1)}/4\}.
\]
Summing the above cost over all levels $j$ therefore upper bounds the expected matching cost in $Q$ produced in this process by
$C_d k|Q|(n_0a_Q)^{-2/d}$.

Let $\mathcal{E}_n'$ be the event where every $Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w$ has at least as many controls as treated points. 
Then we know on $\mathcal{E}_n'$, the matched pairs from the above process form a full injection. 
In addition, a union bound over the probability bound in~\eqref{sharp:eq:deficit} yields 
\[
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_n')\geq 1- \textstyle{\sum_{Q\in \mathcal{Q}_w}} 2k|Q|\exp(-n_0a_Q|Q|/4)
 \geq 1- 2k\exp(-n_0a w^d/4).
\]
On the event $(\mathcal{E}_n')^c$, we discard the above construction and take any injection whose total matching cost is at most $dk/2$.  
Combining the two cases yields~\eqref{sharp:eq:cost-bound}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{sharp:lem:deficit}
For a cube $H\subseteq Q$ of volume $v=|H|=h^d$, let $N_{H,1}$ and $N_{H,0}$ be the
treated and control counts in $H$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{sharp:eq:deficit}
 \mathbb{E}\big[(N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+\big]
 \le2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4),
 \quad \mathbb{P}(N_{H,1}>N_{H,0})\leq 2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Write $M_H=N_{H,1}$ and $K_H=N_{H,0}$. 
We know they are independent of each other, and $M_H\sim \text{Binom}(k,v)$, $K_H\sim \text{Binom}(n_0,p_H)$ for the marginal probability $p_H\ge a_Qv$.
For $t>0$, we know $(M_H-K_H)_+\le M_H e^{t(M_H-K_H)}$, so by independence and the
generating function of the Binomial distribution,  
\$
 \mathbb{E}\big[(M_H-K_H)_+\big] \le kv e^t(1-v+ve^t)^{k-1}
           (1-p_H+p_He^{-t})^{n_0}.
\$
Since $1+u\le e^u$, taking  $t=\log2$, the right-handed side is upper bounded by 
\[
2kv(1+v)^{k-1}(1-p_H/2)^{n_0} \leq  2kv\exp(kv-n_0p_H/2)
 \le2kv\exp(-n_0a_Qv/4),
\]
where the second inequality uses the facts that $v\leq 1$ and $k\le n_0a/4\le n_0a_Q/4$. 
Finally, we know $\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{M_H>K_H\} \leq (N_{H,1}-N_{H,0})_+$, which gives the second inequality.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Similar to the imbalance upper-bound proof, we consider the one-coordinate replacement on the data and the resulting changes in $B_{n_1, n_0}(g)$ to prove its variance bound. Recall 
\[
D=(T_1,\ldots,T_{n_1},C_1,\ldots,C_{n_0}),
\qquad
\sigma_D^\star\in\arg\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal A_{n_1,n_0}}
Q_D(\sigma,A).
\]
Write $S_D=\{\sigma_D^\star(i)\colon 1\leq i\leq n_1\}$ for the selected control set and the imbalance measure
\[
 B_D 
 =\frac1{n_1}\sum_i g(T_i)-\frac1{n_1}\sum_{j\in S_D}g(C_j),
\]
so $B_{n_1,n_2}(g)=B_D$. 

Let $D^{(j)}$ be a one-coordinate replacement of $D$ (recall the definition in Appendix~\ref{app:subsec_imbalance_fixed_theorem}). 
When $j\leq n_1$, a treated sample is replaced by an independent copy from the treated covariate distribution. When $j>n_1$, a control sample is replaced by an independent copy from the control covariate distribution.

First, the optimizer $\sigma_D^\star$ is unique almost surely for both the original dataset $D$ and its one-coordinate replacement since the covariates have a density; see, e.g., the proof of Lemma~\ref{sharp:lem:IBP} for the argument. 

We use the selected-set stability property proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:control_set_stability}: when the cost matrix $c_{ij}=\|T_i-C_j\|^2$ differ in one row or one column and both optima
are unique, their selected-control index sets differ by at most one deletion
and one addition.  
  
We consider two cases for the replacement in $D^{(j)}$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item If a treated point is replaced, then the cost matrices $(c_{ij})$ of $D$ and $(c_{ij}')$ of $D^{(j)}$ differ by one row. The selected-control set differs by at most one deletion and one addition. Thus $|B_{D^{(j)}}-B_D|\le 2R_g/n_1$. 
    \item If a control point is replaced, with abuse of notation we denote the original index as $j\in [n_0]$. Then the treated average doesn't change, and the selected-control set differs by at most one deletion and one addition. We have the identity
    \$
n_1\{B_{D^{(j)}}-B_{D}\}
= 
- \sum_{\ell\in S_{D^{(j)}}}g(C_\ell)
+\sum_{\ell\in S_D}g(C_\ell)
-\mathbf1\{j\in S_{D^{(j)}}\}\{g(C_j')-g(C_j)\}.
\$
The difference of the first two terms are at most $R_g$ due to Lemma~\ref{lem:control_set_stability}. The last term is also bounded by $R_g$.
Therefore 
$|B_{D^{(j)}}-B_D|\le 2R_g/n_1$. 
\end{itemize} 
Combining the two cases and applying Lemma~\ref{lem:ES}, we have 
\[
 \operatorname{{\rm Var}}(B_{n_1,n_2}(g)) \leq \frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^n \mathbb{E}[(B_D - B_{D^{(j)}})^2] \le\frac{n}{2}
 \frac{4R_g^2}{n_1^2} = \frac{2R_g^2(n_1+n_0)}{n_1^2}.
\] 
This proves Proposition~\ref{sharp:prop:variance}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{inf:lem:geometry}
Under Assumptions~\ref{assump:obs-sample} and~\ref{assump:match}, and with the zero convention on $\mathcal I_n^c$, we have 
\[
 \sqrt n\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_L}|B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(g)|\to0,\qquad
 L_n(\hat\sigma_n)=O_P(r_n^2)+o_P(n^{-1}),
\]
where $r_n\asymp(\log n/n)^{1/d}$. In particular, $L_n(\hat\sigma_n)\to0$ and $\sqrt{L_n(\hat\sigma_n)}\log n\to0$ in probability.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
The first claim follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:observational}. For the distance bound, use the event $\mathcal{E}_n$ and the choice of $r_n\asymp (\log n/n)^{1/d}$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:count_stable}. 
Conditional on the treated/control counts, the probability of $\mathcal{E}_n^c$ is at most $n^{-b}$, uniformly over $|n_1/n-\pi|\le\eta$ for some fixed $\eta>0$, by the arguments in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:observational}. On the event $\mathcal{E}_n$, there are abundant control samples within every cubic $Q$, so there exists an injection $\bar\sigma$ so that each $(T_i,C_{\bar\sigma(i)})$ are within the same cubic of diameter $r_n$. On the event  $\mathcal{E}_n \cap \{\|\hat A-A_0\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\lambda_{\min}(A_0)/2\}$  whose probability tends to one, we have 
\[
 \frac{\lambda_{\min}(A_0)}4L_n(\hat\sigma_n)
 \le Q_n(\hat\sigma_n;\hat A)
 =\min_\sigma Q_n(\sigma;\hat A)+\xi_n
 \le Q_n(\bar\sigma;\hat A)+\xi_n
 \le Cr_n^2+\xi_n.
\]
The fact that $\xi_n=o_P(n^{-1})$ then proves the bound on $L_n(\hat\sigma_n)$. The last conclusion follows from $r_n\log n\to0$ and $n^{-1/2}\log n\to0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:transfer}
If $h$ is continuous on $\Omega$ and $L_n(\hat\sigma_n)\to0$ in probability, then
\[
 \frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}|h(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})-h(T_i)|\to0
 \quad\text{in probability}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
For any $u>0$, define  $\omega_h(u)=\sup_{\|x-y\|\le u}|h(x)-h(y)|$. Then, the displayed average is at most $\omega_h(u)+2\|h\|_\infty L_n(\hat\sigma_n)/u^2$. 
For every fixed $u>0$, letting $n\to\infty$ yields 
\$
 \frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}|h(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})-h(T_i)| \leq \omega_h(u) + o_P(1). 
\$
Since $h$ is continuous on the compact set $\Omega$, we know $\lim_{u\downarrow 0} \omega_h(u)=0$. Hence by the arbitrariness of $u>0$ we must have the desired result.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We now begin the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ATT}. 
\paragraph{Asymptotic normality for $\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}$.} Write $b_i=\mu_0(T_i)-\mu_0(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})$,
$\varepsilon_{1,i}=Y_{1,i}-\mu_1(T_i)$,
$\varepsilon_{0,j}=Y_{0,j}-\mu_0(C_j)$, and
$\eta_i=\varepsilon_{1,i}-\varepsilon_{0,\hat\sigma_n(i)}$.
Then $R_i=\tau(T_i)+b_i+\eta_i$, which yields the decomposition
\@\label{eq:ATT-decomp}
 \sqrt {n_1}(\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}-\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}})
 = \underbrace{\frac1{\sqrt {n_1}}\sum_i\{\tau(T_i)-\theta\}}_{\textnormal{(i)}}
 +\underbrace{\sqrt {n_1} B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(\mu_0)}_{\textnormal{(ii)}}
 +\underbrace{\frac1{\sqrt {n_1}}\sum_i\eta_i}_{\textnormal{(iii)}}.
\@

Conditional on $\mathcal{G}_n$, the residuals of distinct observations are independent and mean zero. This follows from i.i.d. sampling, conditioning on their individual covariates and labels, and unconfoundedness. Since the matching is an injection, different $\eta_i$ involve disjoint observations. Thus one can show that conditional on $\mathcal{G}_n$, the $\eta_i$'s are conditionally independent and mean zero, with conditional variances $q_i=\sigma_1^2(T_i)+\sigma_0^2(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})$. 
Since $|x-y|^4\le8(|x|^4+|y|^4)$ for any $x,y\in \mathbb{R}$, the conditional fourth moment (and hence the third moment) is uniformly bounded.  
  
Lemma~\ref{lem:transfer} applied to $\sigma_0^2(\cdot)$ gives 
\$
\frac{1}{n_1}\sum_i\sigma_0^2(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})=\frac{1}{n_1}\sum_i\sigma_0^2(T_i) +o_P(1)
=  \mathbb{E}[\sigma_0^2(T_i)] +o_P(1).
\$ 
The last identity is because conditional on the treated and control group sizes, $(T_1,\dots,T_{n_1})$ are i.i.d.~random variables. 
Thus we have $\frac{1}{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1} q_i = v_\epsilon + o_P(1)$, where recall $v_\epsilon = \mathbb{E}_1\{\sigma_1^2(X)+\sigma_0^2(X)\}$. 

For the first term in \eqref{eq:ATT-decomp}, write
\[
 \frac1{\sqrt{n_1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}\{\tau(T_i)-\theta\}
 =\sqrt{\frac n{n_1}}\frac1{\sqrt n}
       \sum_{k=1}^n Z_k\{\tau(X_k)-\theta\}.
\]
The summands on the right are i.i.d. and bounded, have mean zero and variance $\pi v_\tau$. The i.i.d. CLT and $n_1/n\to\pi$ give a $N(0,v_\tau)$ limit, including $v_\tau=0$. 

Finally, the middle term in \eqref{eq:ATT-decomp} obeys 
\$
\sqrt{n_1} B_{n,\hat\sigma_n}(\mu_0) = o_P(1) 
\$
by Theorem~\ref{thm:observational}, since $\mu_0$ belongs to a fixed $\mathcal F_L$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:conditional-CLT} on the original probability space, and using the fact that term (iii) is asymptotically normal conditional on $\mathcal{G}_n$ while the other two terms are $\mathcal{G}_n$-measurable, we obtain the the normal limit with variance $v_\tau+v_\epsilon$. In particular, $\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}\to\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}$.

\paragraph{Convergence of $\hat{V}_{\textnormal{ATT}}$.}
Let $a_i=\tau(T_i)+b_i$, and recall that $b_i = \mu_0(T_i) - \mu_0(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})$. These quantities are uniformly bounded. 
Lipschitz continuity of $\mu_0$ gives
${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i b_i^2\le||\nabla\mu_0||_\infty^2L_D(\hat\sigma_n)\to0$ in probability. 
Then by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have 
${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i\{a_i^2-\tau(T_i)^2\}\to0$. 
Since conditional on treatment indicators, the treated samples are i.i.d.~from the treated group distribution, we know  ${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i a_i^2\to{\mathbb E}\{\tau(X)^2\mid Z=1\}$.

Recall that $\eta_i = \varepsilon_{1,i} - \varepsilon_{0,\hat\sigma_n(i)}$, and $q_i = \sigma_1^2(T_i)+\sigma_0^2(C_{\hat\sigma_n(i)})$. 
Conditional on $\mathcal{G}_n$, we know ${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i(\eta_i^2-q_i)$ has mean zero and variance at most $C/{n_1}$ by the fourth-moment bound. For every fixed $\epsilon>0$, we have 
\$
\mathbb{P}\Big( {n_1}^{-1}\sum_i(\eta_i^2-q_i)\geq \epsilon\Big) \leq \mathbb{E}\big[\min\{1,C/({n_1}\epsilon^2)\} \big] + \mathbb{P}(\mathcal{I}_n^c).
\$
up to the vanishing probability of $\mathcal I_n^c$. 
The right-handed side tends to zero because ${n_1}\to\infty$ in probability and the minimum is bounded by one. Similarly, ${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i a_i\eta_i$ has conditional mean zero and variance at most $C/{n_1}$, because the $a_i$ and $q_i$ are bounded, and the same averaging argument applies. Expanding $R_i^2=a_i^2+2a_i\eta_i+\eta_i^2$ gives
${n_1}^{-1}\sum_iR_i^2\to{\mathbb E}\{\tau(X)^2\mid Z=1\}+v_\epsilon$.
Finally,
$\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}=\{{n_1}/({n_1}-1)\}\{{n_1}^{-1}\sum_iR_i^2-\hat\theta^2\}\to v_\tau+v_\epsilon$.
Thus by Slutsky's theorem, we have the desired coverage of the Wald-CI $\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}/{n_1}}$. 

\paragraph{Validity of bootstrap inference.}
We now prove the validity of the matched-pair bootstrap inference. 
We first check empirical moments of the original paired differences, then analyze the bootstrap draws.


The conditional fourth moment of every $R_i$ is bounded by a common constant, from the bounded conditional means and the outcome assumption. Hence $M_{4n}={n_1}^{-1}\sum_i|R_i|^4=O_P(1)$ by Markov's inequality. 
By Jensen's inequality, we have $|\hat\theta|_{\textnormal{ATT}}^4\le M_{4n}$, and so ${n_1}^{-1}\sum_i |R_i-\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}|^4\le16M_{4n}=O_p(1)$. Write $M_{kn}^{c}={n_1}^{-1}\sum_i |R_i-\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}|^k$ for $k=3,4$. 
By the fourth-moment condition and Jensen's inequality, we know $M_{kn}^{c} = O_P(1)$ for $k=3,4$. The centered empirical variance is $s_n^2=\frac{1}{n_1}\sum_i(R_i-\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}})^2= \frac{ n_1 -1}{n_1} \hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}\to V$.

Conditional on the full observed data and the original matching,
let $I_1^*,\ldots,I_{n_1}^*$ be independent and uniform on
$\{1,\ldots,n_1\}$. Let $\mathbb{P}^*$ and $\mathbb{E}^*$ denote the probability and expectation under the bootstrap resampling law, conditional on the data. Define
\[
W_i^*=R_{I_i^*}-\widehat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}},
\qquad
S_n^*=\frac1{\sqrt{n_1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}W_i^*
=\sqrt{n_1}
(\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*-\hat\theta_{\textnormal{ATT}}).
\]
Under the conditional bootstrap resampling draw, the $W_i^*$ are i.i.d.
and satisfy
\[
\mathbb{E}^*[W_b^*]=0,\qquad
\mathbb{E}^*[(W_b^*)^2]=s_n^2,\qquad
\mathbb{E}^*[|W_b^*|^3]=M_{3n}^c.
\]

On the event $\{n_1\ge2,\ s_n>0\}$, the Berry--Esseen
inequality \citep[Theorem~3.4.17]{Durrett2019}, applied to
this conditional distribution, gives
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
\big|
\mathbb{P}^*\{S_n^*/s_n\le x\}-\Phi(x)
\big|
\le
\frac{3M_{3n}^c}{\sqrt{n_1}s_n^3}
=o_P(1).
\]
The last equality follows from $M_{3n}^c=O_P(1)$,
$n_1\to\infty$ in probability, and $s_n^2\to V>0$
in probability. These conditions also imply that the
event on which the inequality is applied has probability
tending to one.

To replace $s_n$ by $\sqrt V$, note that for any $s,t>0$,
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\Phi(x/s)-\Phi(x/t)\big|
\le C_\Phi\big|\log(s/t) \big|,
\qquad
C_\Phi=\sup_{y\in\mathbb R}|y\phi(y)|<\infty,
\]
where $\phi(\cdot)$ is the standard normal density function. This follows
by differentiating $\Phi(xe^{-u})$ with respect to $u$
and integrating between $\log s$ and $\log t$.
Consequently,
\$
&\sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
\left|
\mathbb{P}^*\{S_n^*\le x\}-\Phi(x/\sqrt V)
\right| \le
\frac{3M_{3n}^c}{\sqrt{n_1}s_n^3}+C_\Phi|\log(s_n/\sqrt V)|
\xrightarrow{P}0.
\$
                                    
It remains to justify the random denominator used in the bootstrap
$t$-statistic. Write
\[
 \overline W_n^*  = \frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1} W_i^*
 =
 \hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*
 -\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}},
 \qquad
 A_n^* = \frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}(W_i^*)^2 ,\qquad 
 \hat V_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*
 =
 \frac{n_1}{n_1-1}
 \left\{
 A_n^*-(\overline W_n^*)^2
 \right\}.
\]
Conditional on the observed data, we know 
\[
 \mathbb{E}^* [A_n^*]=s_n^2,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Var}^*(A_n^*)
 \le
 \frac{M^c_{4n}}{n_1},
 \qquad
 \mathbb E^*\{(\overline W_n^*)^2\}
 =
 \frac{s_n^2}{n_1}.
\]
Consequently, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$,
conditional Chebyshev's and Markov's inequalities give
\[
 \mathbb{P}^*
 \left(
 |A_n^*-s_n^2|>\varepsilon
 \right)
 \le
 \frac{M^c_{4n}}{n_1\varepsilon^2},
 \qquad
 \mathbb{P}^*
 \left(
 (\overline W_n^*)^2>\varepsilon
 \right)
 \le
 \frac{s_n^2}{n_1\varepsilon}.
\]
Since
$M^c_{4n}=O_{P}(1)$,
$s_n^2 = V_{\textnormal{ATT}}+o_P(1)$, and
$n_1\to\infty$ in probability, it follows that $\mathbb{P}^* ( |\hat{V}_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*-s_n^2|>\varepsilon
  )\to 0$ in probability, 
 and in particular,
\[
 \mathbb{P}^*\big(
 | {\hat V_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*}/{s_n^2}-1
 |>\varepsilon \big)
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,
\]
and therefore 
$ \mathbb{P}^*(\hat{V}_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*=0)
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0$. 
Now let 
 $Y_n^*=\frac{S_n^*}{s_n}$ and 
 $R_n^*=
 \sqrt{\hat{V}_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*}/s_n$ 
on the event $\hat{V}_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*>0$.
The conditional Berry--Esseen bound above gives
\[
 \sup_x
 \left|
 \mathbb{P}^*(Y_n^*\le x)-\Phi(x)
 \right|
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,
\]
while the previous arguments give  
 $\mathbb{P}^*(|R_n^*-1|>\varepsilon)
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0$ for any $\varepsilon>0$. 
Hence   Slutsky's theorem yields
\[
 \sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
 \left|
 \mathbb{P}^* \{  {Y_n^*}/{R_n^*}\le x \}
 -\Phi(x)
 \right|
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.
\]
Since $Y_n^*/R_n^*=T^{*(1)}$ whenever
$\hat{V}_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}^*>0$, and the complementary event has
conditional probability tending to zero in probability, we know that 
\$
 \sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
 \big|
 \mathbb{P}^*\{T^{*(1)}\le x\}
 -\Phi(x)
 \big| \xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0. 
\$

We then prove the validity of the bootstrap confidence interval. 
Fix $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and let $B=B_n\to\infty$ be a
deterministic sequence of positive integers. Write 
$\hat{\mathrm{se}}_n=\sqrt{\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}/n_1}$ and 
$T_n=\frac{\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}-\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}}
{\hat{\mathrm{se}}_n}$. 
The sampling central limit theorem and variance consistency
proved above give 
$T_n\Rightarrow N(0,1)$. 
Let
\[
F_n^*(t)=\mathbb{P}^*\{T^{*(1)}\le t\},
\qquad t\in\mathbb R.
\]
The studentized bootstrap result in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ATT} gives 
$\sup_{t\in\mathbb R}|F_n^*(t)-\Phi(t)|
\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0$. 
Here $\mathbb{P}^*$ denotes probability over the bootstrap
draws conditional on the full observed data. In what follows, the probability $\mathbb{P}$ includes both the
original sampling and the
bootstrap sampling randomness. 
 
Define the empirical distribution function
\[
\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)
=
\frac1{B_n}\sum_{b=1}^{B_n}
\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{T^{*(b)}\le t\}.
\]
For each fixed $t$, conditional on the data, we know
\[
\mathbb{E}^*[\hat{F}_{n,B_n}^*(t)]=F_n^*(t),
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}^*\{\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)\}
=
\frac{F_n^*(t)\{1-F_n^*(t)\}}{B_n}
\le\frac1{4B_n}.
\]
For every $\varepsilon>0$, conditional Chebyshev's
inequality and averaging over the original data give
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{P}\left\{
|\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)-F_n^*(t)|>\varepsilon
\right\} = 
\mathbb{E}\left[
\mathbb{P}^*\left\{
|\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)-F_n^*(t)|>\varepsilon
\right\}
\right] \le\frac1{4B_n\varepsilon^2} \to 0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Combining this with $|F_n^*(t)-\Phi(t)|=o_P(1)$ yields
\[
\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\Phi(t)
\qquad\text{for every fixed }t\in\mathbb R.
\]

For $q\in(0,1)$, recall that 
$c_q^*=\inf\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(t)\ge q\}$,
where
$z_q=\Phi^{-1}(q)$. 
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of the empirical
quantile,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{P}\{|c_q^*-z_q|>\varepsilon\}
\le{}&
\mathbb{P}\{\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(z_q-\varepsilon)\ge q\} +
\mathbb{P}\{\hat F_{n,B_n}^*(z_q+\varepsilon)<q\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since 
$\Phi(z_q-\varepsilon)<q<\Phi(z_q+\varepsilon)$, 
both probabilities tend to zero by the pointwise
convergence established before. Thus 
$c_q^*- z_q = o_P(1)$. 
In particular, this holds jointly for
$q=\alpha/2$ and $q=1-\alpha/2$.
   
Consider
\[
\mathrm{CI}^{\mathrm{boot}}_{n,1-\alpha}
=
\big[ 
\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}
-c_{1-\alpha/2}^*\widehat{\mathrm{se}}_n,
\quad
\hat\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}
-c_{\alpha/2}^*\widehat{\mathrm{se}}_n\big].
\]
On the event $\mathcal{I}_n\cap\{\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}>0\}$,
its coverage event is  
$\{
\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}\in
\mathrm{CI}^{\mathrm{boot}}_{n,1-\alpha}
\}=\{
c_{\alpha/2}^*\le T_n\le c_{1-\alpha/2}^*\}$. 
Note that $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{I}_n\cap\{\hat V_{\textnormal{ATT}}>0\})\to 1$. 
Taking the confidence interval to be $\mathbb{R}$ on its
complement therefore gives
\[
\mathbb{P}\left\{
\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}\in
\mathrm{CI}^{\mathrm{boot}}_{n,1-\alpha}
\right\}
=
\mathbb{P}\left\{
c_{\alpha/2}^*\le T_n\le c_{1-\alpha/2}^*
\right\}
+o(1).
\]

Let $z_\ell=z_{\alpha/2}$ and $z_u=z_{1-\alpha/2}$.
For $0<\delta<(z_u-z_\ell)/2$, define 
$E_{n,\delta}
=
 \{
|c_{\alpha/2}^*-z_\ell|\le\delta,~
|c_{1-\alpha/2}^*-z_u|\le\delta
 \}$. 
The quantile convergence gives
$\mathbb{P}(E_{n,\delta}^c)\to0$. A decomposition gives 
                             
    
                                        
           
                                               
           
                                         
    
               
\$
&\mathbb{P}\{z_\ell+\delta\le T_n\le z_u-\delta\}
-\mathbb{P}(E_{n,\delta}^c)\leq
\mathbb{P}\{c_{\alpha/2}^*\le T_n\le c_{1-\alpha/2}^*\} \le
\mathbb{P}\{z_\ell-\delta\le T_n\le z_u+\delta\}
+\mathbb{P}(E_{n,\delta}^c).
\$
Using $T_n\Rightarrow N(0,1)$, the limiting lower and upper
bounds are, respectively, 
$\Phi(z_u-\delta)-\Phi(z_\ell+\delta)$ 
and
$\Phi(z_u+\delta)-\Phi(z_\ell-\delta)$. 
Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ makes both bounds equal to
$\Phi(z_u)-\Phi(z_\ell)=1-\alpha$. Hence
\[
\mathbb{P}\big\{
\theta_{{\textnormal{ATT}}}\in
\mathrm{CI}^{\mathrm{boot}}_{n,1-\alpha}
\big\} \to 1-\alpha,
\]
and we complete the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:ATT}.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{cdf32:lem:crossing}
Fix $m\ge2$ and CDFs $F_i,G_i$, $i=1,\ldots,m$. Let $V_i$ be
independent uniform variables on $(0,1)$, and define
\[
A_i=F_i^{\leftarrow}(V_i),\qquad B_i=G_i^{\leftarrow}(V_i),\qquad
e_i(y)=\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{A_i\le y\}-\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{B_i\le y\},\qquad
\delta=\frac1m\sum_i\|F_i-G_i\|_\infty.
\]
For each $r>0$, there exists a constant $C_r$ independent of $m$ and of these CDFs  
such that, with probability at least $1-C_r m^{-r}$, both inequalities hold:
\begin{align}
\sup_y\left|\frac1{\sqrt m}\sum_i\{e_i(y)-\mathbb Ee_i(y)\}\right|
&\le C_r\left\{\sqrt{\delta\log m}+\frac{\log m}{\sqrt m}\right\},
\label{cdf32:eq:crossing-centered}\\
\sup_y\frac1m\sum_i e_i(y)^2
&\le\delta+C_r\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\delta\log m}{m}}
                          +\frac{\log m}{m}\right\}.
\label{cdf32:eq:crossing-square}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{cdf32:lem:resample}
Fix any $m\ge2$ pairs of real numbers $(A_i,B_i)$ and define $e_i(y)$ as
in Lemma~\ref{cdf32:lem:crossing}. Let 
\[
\bar e_m(y)=\frac1m\sum_i e_i(y),\qquad
\kappa_m=\sup_y\frac1m\sum_i e_i(y)^2.
\]
For independent uniform indices $I_1^*,\ldots,I_m^*$ on $\{1,\ldots,m\}$,
with conditional probability at least $1-C_r m^{-r}$,
\begin{equation}\label{cdf32:eq:resampling-bound}
\sup_y\left|\frac1{\sqrt m}\sum_{i=1}^m
                   \{e_{I_i^*}(y)-\bar e_m(y)\}\right|
\le C_r\left\{\sqrt{\kappa_m\log m}+\frac{\log m}{\sqrt m}\right\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:chernoff}
If $X$ is binomial with mean $\mu$, then $\mathbb{P}\{X\le(1-\epsilon)\mu\}\le\exp(-\epsilon^2\mu/2)$ for $0<\epsilon<1$, and $\mathbb{P}\{X\ge(1+\epsilon)\mu\}\le\exp\{-\epsilon^2\mu/(2+\epsilon)\}$ for $\epsilon>0$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:ES}
For independent coordinates $Z=(Z_1,\ldots,Z_s)$ and square-integrable $H(Z)$, let $Z^{(j)}$ replace $Z_j$ by an independent copy. Then $\operatorname{{\rm Var}} (H)\le\frac12\sum_j\mathbb{E}[\{H(Z)-H(Z^{(j)})\}^2]$.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:Taylor}
For $x,y\in\Omega$, $g(x)-g(y)=\nabla g(x)^\top(x-y)+R_g(x,y)$, where $|R_g(x,y)|\le 1/2\cdot||\nabla^2 g||_\infty||x-y||^2$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:envelope}
Let $\{a_j\}_{j=1}^J$ and $\{b_j\}_{j=1}^J$ be fixed real numbers for some fixed integer $J<\infty$. 
Define $V(t)=\min_{j\in J}(a_j+t b_j)$. 
Then $V$ is continuous, concave, piecewise affine, and absolutely continuous on bounded intervals. If $j_0$ is a minimizer at $t=0$, i.e., $a_{j_0}=V(0)$, then for every $h>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:secants}
 \frac{V(h)-V(0)}h\le b_{j_0}\le\frac{V(0)-V(-h)}h.
\end{equation}
Finally, for every $t_0\in \mathbb{R}$ at which $V$ is differentiable, we have $b_j = V'(t_0)$ for every $j\in \mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{k \in J} \{a_k+t_0b_k\}$.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Since each $t\mapsto a_j+tb_j$ function is affine, the minimum of finitely many such affine functions is concave and continuous. Also, as each of them is a straight line, since $J<\infty$, there are only finitely many possible intersection points between distinct nonparallel lines (identical lines cause no difficulty). The function $V$ is therefore piecewise affine. 
Equation~\eqref{eq:secants} follows from the fact that $V(h)\le a_{j_0}+hb_{j_0}$ and $V(-h)\le a_{j_0}-hb_{j_0}$ due to the definition of $V$. 
To prove the last claim, fix any $t_0\in \mathbb{R}$ at which $V$ is differentiable, and take any $j\in \mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{k\in J}\{a_k+t_0b_k\}$. Then by the definition of $V$, for every $h\in \mathbb{R}$, 
\$
V(t_0+h)\leq a_j + (t_0+h) b_j  = V(t_0)+hb_j.
\$
For $h>0$, dividing both sides by $h$ and take the limit $h\to 0$, by differentiability we know $V'(t_0)\leq b_j$. 
For $h<0$, dividing both sides by $h$ changes the direction of the inequality; so taking the limit $h\to 0$, by differentiability we know $V'(t_0)\geq b_j$. Combining the two directions, we obtain $V'(t_0)=b_j$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif:bd}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be independent random elements and let
$W=w(X_1,\ldots,X_n)$ for a bounded measurable real function $w$.
For each $j$, let $X^{(j)}$ replace $X_j$ by an independent copy from
its own distribution. Suppose there are deterministic $c_j\ge0$ such that
$|w(X)-w(X^{(j)})|\le c_j$ almost surely for each $j$.
If $v_c=\sum_{j=1}^n c_j^2>0$, then for every $u>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif:bd}
 \mathbb P(|W-\mathbb{E}[W]|>u)
 \le2\exp(-2u^2/v_c).
\end{equation}
If $v_c=0$, then $W=\mathbb{E}[W]$ almost surely.
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
To justify this almost-sure formulation, write $X_{-j}=(X_i)_{i\ne j}$
and $P_j=\mathcal L(X_j)$.
By Fubini, for almost every $x_{-j}$, the replacement
assumption gives
\[
|w(x_{-j},x_j)-w(x_{-j},x'_j)|\le c_j
\quad\text{for }(P_j\otimes P_j)\text{-almost every }
(x_j,x'_j).
\]
Here $w(x_{-j},x_j)$ means that $x_j$ is inserted in
coordinate $j$. Consequently, the conditional essential
range of $W$ given $X_{-j}$ has length at most $c_j$:
otherwise, two sets of values separated by more than $c_j$
would each have positive conditional probability, contrary
to the displayed bound and conditional independence of
the two draws.
The proof of the cited theorem applies with these essential
ranges and gives the asserted tail bound.
If $v_c=0$, the Efron--Stein inequality gives
$\operatorname{Var}(W)=0$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif:entropy}
For the class $\mathcal F_L$ on $[0,1]^d$, there is a constant $C$
depending only on $d,L$ such that, for every $0<\varepsilon\le1$, a
finite set $\mathcal N_\varepsilon\subset\mathcal F_L$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unif:entropy}
 \sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}\min_{f\in\mathcal N_\varepsilon}
          \|g-f\|_\infty\le\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 \log|\mathcal N_\varepsilon|
 \le C\varepsilon^{-d/2}\log(C/\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\mathcal F_L$ has a countable dense subset in the sup norm.
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:unif:chain}
Let $1\le d<4$. For every $n$, let $\{Z_n(g):g\in\mathcal F_L\}$ be a real
process with measurable coordinates and almost surely continuous paths
under $\|\cdot\|_\infty$.
Suppose a fixed constant $0<K<\infty$ satisfies
\[
 \mathbb P(|Z_n(g)-Z_n(f)|>u)
 \le2\exp\{-u^2/(K\|g-f\|_\infty^2)\}
\]
for every $n$, distinct $g,f\in\mathcal F_L$, and $u>0$.
Then whenever $Z_n(g)=o_P(1)$ for every fixed $g$, we have  
 $\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|Z_n(g)|\to 0$ in probability.
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
For $\delta>0$, define
\[
\omega_n(\delta)
=
\sup_{ g,f\in\mathcal F_L, \|g-f\|_\infty<\delta}
|Z_n(g)-Z_n(f)|.
\]
The countable dense subset given in 
Lemma~\ref{lem:unif:entropy}, together with sample-path
continuity, gives measurable versions of this supremum
and of $\sup_g|Z_n(g)|$.

The sub-Gaussian increment bound and the entropy-integral
maximal inequality of
\citet[Theorem~2.2.4]{VW1996} imply
\[
\sup_n\mathbb{E}\omega_n(\delta)
\le
C\int_0^\delta
\sqrt{1+\log N(u,\mathcal F_L,\|\cdot\|_\infty)}\,du
\to 0 
\]
as $\delta\downarrow 0$. 
where the last limit follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:unif:entropy} and $d<4$.

Fix $\delta>0$ and choose a deterministic finite
$\delta/2$-net $\mathcal N_\delta\subset\mathcal F_L$.
For every sample path,
\[
\sup_{g\in\mathcal F_L}|Z_n(g)|
\le
\max_{f\in\mathcal N_\delta}|Z_n(f)|+\omega_n(\delta).
\]
Consequently, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
\mathbb P\!\left\{\sup_g|Z_n(g)|>\varepsilon\right\}
\le
\sum_{f\in\mathcal N_\delta}
\mathbb P\{|Z_n(f)|>\varepsilon/2\}
+
\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\omega_n(\delta).
\]
For any fixed $\delta$, the finite sum tends to zero by
pointwise convergence. Taking $\limsup_{n\to\infty}$
and then letting $\delta\downarrow0$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{lem:expect_var_bd}
    If a real random variable $Z$ has mean $\mu>0$ and variance at most $v$, then $\mathbb{P}(Z\geq \mu/2) \geq \mu^2/(4v+\mu^2)$.
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
If $v=0$ the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise, for any $t>0$ and $b\geq 0$, on the event $\{Z-\mu\leq -t\}$ we have $(Z-\mu-b)^2 \geq (t+b)^2$. Thus by Markov's inequality, we have $\mathbb{P}(Z-\mu\leq -t) \leq (v+b^2)/(t+b)^2$. Taking $b=v/t$  gives $\mathbb{P}(Z-\mu\leq -t)\leq (v+v^2/t^2)/(t+v/t)^2$, hence $\mathbb{P}(Z\geq \mu/2)\geq 1- (v+v^2/t^2)/(t+v/t)^2 = 1-v/(v+t^2) = t^2/(v+t^2)$. Then, taking $t=\mu/2$ gives the desired bound.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-30: body_md -->
\label{lem:Levy}
If characteristic functions of probability laws on $\mathbb{R}^k$ converge pointwise to a characteristic function continuous at zero, the corresponding laws converge weakly to that law. Conversely, weak convergence implies pointwise convergence of characteristic functions.
<!-- end lemma-30 -->
<!-- lemma-31: body_md -->
\label{lem:conditional-CLT}
Let $n_1=n_1(n)\in \mathbb{N}^+$ be $\mathcal{G}_n$-measurable  with ${n_1}\to\infty$ in probability. Conditional on $\mathcal{G}_n$, suppose the row $\eta_{n1},\ldots,\eta_{n,n_1}$ is independent and centered, with $\mathbb{E}(|\eta_{ni}|^3\mid\mathcal{G}_n)\le K$ for a fixed constant $K$, $1\leq i\leq n_1$. If
$v_n={n_1}^{-1}\sum_i{\mathbb E}(\eta_{ni}^2\mid\mathcal{G}_n)\to v\ge0$ in probability, then for each fixed $u\in \mathbb{R}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cond-cf}
 {\mathbb E}\left[ e^{iu {n_1}^{-1/2}\sum_i\eta_{ni}}{\,|\,} |\mathcal{G}_n\right]
 \to \exp(-u^2v/2)
 \quad\text{in probability and }L^1.
\end{equation}
If $A_n$ is $\mathcal{G}_n$-measurable and $A_n\Rightarrow N(0,w)$, then $A_n+{n_1}^{-1/2}\sum_i\eta_{ni}\Rightarrow N(0,w+v)$.
<!-- end lemma-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Write 
$S_n=n_1^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}\eta_{ni}$, 
$\varphi_n(u)=\mathbb{E}(e^{iuS_n}\mid\mathcal G_n)$. 
For every $\varepsilon>0$, the third-moment bound gives
\[
\frac1{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}
\mathbb{E}\!\left[
\eta_{ni}^2
\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{|\eta_{ni}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{n_1}\}
\,\middle|\,\mathcal G_n
\right]
\le \frac{K}{\varepsilon\sqrt{n_1}}.
\]

Suppose first that $v>0$. From any subsequence, select a
further subsequence along which $n_1\to\infty$ and $v_n\to v$
almost surely. For almost every realization, the conditional
laws along this further subsequence satisfy the conditions
of the Lindeberg--Feller theorem
\citep[Theorem~3.4.10]{Durrett2019}.
Applying it to these independent rows gives
$\varphi_n(u)\to e^{-u^2v/2}$ almost surely along the further
subsequence, for every fixed $u\in\mathbb R$.
Thus the convergence holds in probability along the original
sequence.

If $v=0$, conditional Cauchy--Schwarz gives directly
$|\varphi_n(u)-1|
\le |u|\,\mathbb{E}(|S_n|\mid\mathcal G_n)
\le |u|\sqrt{v_n} = o_P(1)$.
In either case, $|\varphi_n(u)-e^{-u^2v/2}|\le2$,
so the convergence also holds in $L^1$.

Finally, since $A_n$ is $\mathcal G_n$-measurable,
we know 
$\mathbb{E} [e^{iu(A_n+S_n)}]=\mathbb{E}\{e^{iuA_n}\varphi_n(u)\} =e^{-u^2v/2}\mathbb{E} [e^{iuA_n}]+o(1) \to e^{-u^2(v+w)/2}$.  
The error in the second equality is bounded in absolute
value by $\mathbb{E}|\varphi_n(u)-e^{-u^2v/2}|$.
The last assertion follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:Levy}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
For each fixed threshold $y$, the inverse-CDF identity gives
\[
\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{A_i\le y\}=\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{V_i\le F_i(y)\},\qquad
\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{B_i\le y\}=\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{V_i\le G_i(y)\}.
\]
Their difference is nonzero if and only if $V_i$ lies between the two CDF
values, apart from endpoints of probability zero. Therefore
\[
\mathbb{E}[e_i(y)]=F_i(y)-G_i(y),\qquad
\mathbb{E}[e_i(y)^2]=|F_i(y)-G_i(y)|.
\]
The centered $e_i(y)$ are bounded by two and their variance sum is at most
$m\delta$. The $e_i(y)^2$ are indicators, with both mean sum and variance
sum at most $m\delta$.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{First, suppose the CDFs are continuous.}
Let $J(y)=(2m)^{-1}\sum_i\{F_i(y)+G_i(y)\}$ and put $K=m^2$.
Choose increasing points $y_j$ with $J(y_j)=j/K$, $1\le j<K$, and add
$y_0=-\infty$, $y_K=+\infty$. 
The existence of such points follows from continuity. Each interval $(y_j,y_{j+1}]$ has $J$-mass $1/K$.
Let
\[
N_j=\sum_{i=1}^m
 \{\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{A_i\in(y_j,y_{j+1}]\}
  +\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{B_i\in(y_j,y_{j+1}]\}\}.
\]
Then $\mathbb{E}[N_j]=2m/K$. The contributions for different $i$ are independent
and lie in $[0,2]$. Since $x^2\le2x$ on $[0,2]$, their variance sum is at
most $4m/K$. Their centered versions are bounded by two.

Apply \eqref{cdf32:eq:bernstein} at every grid point to the centered sums
of $e_i(y_j)$ and of $e_i(y_j)^2$, and to each $N_j-2m/K$.
There are at most $3(K+1)$ quantities. With $t=(r+4)\log m$, a union
bound gives failure probability at most
$6(K+1)m^{-(r+4)}\le C_r m^{-r}$. On its complement,
\begin{align*}
\max_j\left|\sum_i\{e_i(y_j)-\mathbb{E}[e_i(y_j)]\}\right|
&\le C_r\{\sqrt{m\delta\log m}+\log m\},\\
\max_j\sum_i e_i(y_j)^2
&\le m\delta+C_r\{\sqrt{m\delta\log m}+\log m\},\\
\max_{j<K}N_j
&\le\frac{2m}{K}
  +C_r\left\{\sqrt{\frac{m\log m}{K}}+\log m\right\}.
\end{align*}
For $y_j\le y\le y_{j+1}$, an indicator can change only if the corresponding
observation belongs to $(y_j,y_{j+1}]$. Consequently,
\[
\sum_i|e_i(y)-e_i(y_j)|\le N_j,
\qquad
\sum_i|\mathbb{E}[e_i(y)]-\mathbb{E}[e_i(y_j)]|\le 2m/K.
\]
Thus the centered sum at any threshold differs from its value at a grid
point by at most $N_j+2m/K$. Also, because
$|a^2-b^2|\le2|a-b|$ for $a,b\in\{-1,0,1\}$,
\[
\sum_i e_i(y)^2\le\sum_i e_i(y_j)^2+2N_j.
\]
Divide these bounds by $\sqrt m$ and $m$, respectively. With $K=m^2$, the
additional terms are bounded by $C_r\log m/\sqrt m$ and
$C_r\log m/m$. Enlarging $C_r$ proves
\eqref{cdf32:eq:crossing-centered}--\eqref{cdf32:eq:crossing-square}, including
the two tail intervals. The finite grid points have no outcome mass in
this continuous case.

\medskip
\noindent\textit{Now allow jumps in the CDFs.}
Fix $m$ and the CDFs. For $\epsilon>0$ define
\[
F_{i,\epsilon}(y)=\int_0^1 F_i(y+\epsilon v)\,dv,
\qquad
G_{i,\epsilon}(y)=\int_0^1 G_i(y+\epsilon v)\,dv.
\]
For any proper CDF $F$, the corresponding $F_\epsilon$ is the law of
$X-\epsilon V$ for independent $X\sim F$ and $V\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)$.
Conditionally on $X$, this law has a density bounded by $1/\epsilon$, so
$F_\epsilon$ is continuous. Monotonicity and right continuity give
\begin{equation}\label{cdf32:eq:smoothing}
F(y)\le F_\epsilon(y)\le F(y+\epsilon),\qquad
F_\epsilon(y)\to F(y)\quad\text{for every }y.
\end{equation}
If $F\le G$, the set where $G\ge v$ contains the set where $F\ge v$;
taking their infima gives $G^{\leftarrow}(v)\le F^{\leftarrow}(v)$.
Apply this to the two inequalities in \eqref{cdf32:eq:smoothing} to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{cdf32:eq:inverse-sandwich}
F^{\leftarrow}(v)-\epsilon
\le F_\epsilon^{\leftarrow}(v)\le F^{\leftarrow}(v),
\qquad 0<v<1.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
$\|F_{i,\epsilon}-G_{i,\epsilon}\|_\infty
 \le\|F_i-G_i\|_\infty$ by the integral triangle inequality.

Using the original uniforms, define
$A_i^\epsilon=F_{i,\epsilon}^{\leftarrow}(V_i)$ and
$B_i^\epsilon=G_{i,\epsilon}^{\leftarrow}(V_i)$, and let
$e_i^\epsilon(y)$ be their indicator differences. The continuous-case bounds
hold with the original $\delta$ and the same constants for every $\epsilon$.
Equation \eqref{cdf32:eq:inverse-sandwich} implies
$A_i-\epsilon\le A_i^\epsilon\le A_i$ and similarly for $B_i$.
For every fixed $y$, it follows that 
$\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{A_i^\epsilon\le y\}\to \operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{A_i\le y\}$, and 
$\operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{B_i^\epsilon\le y\}\to \operatorname{\mathds{1}}\{B_i\le y\}$. 
The expectations of $e_i^\epsilon(y)$
also converge to those of $e_i(y)$ by \eqref{cdf32:eq:smoothing}.

For these fixed $m$ and CDFs, let $a$ and $b$ denote the deterministic
right sides of \eqref{cdf32:eq:crossing-centered} and
\eqref{cdf32:eq:crossing-square}. All the paths involved are right-continuous,
so their suprema equal the suprema over rational thresholds. If either
original supremum exceeds its bound, a rational threshold witnesses a strict
violation. Along any deterministic $\epsilon_l\downarrow0$, that same strict
violation persists for all sufficiently large $l$. Writing $E$ and $E_l$
for the unions of the two failure events in the original and approximating
samples, respectively, gives
\[
\operatorname{\mathds{1}}_E\le\liminf_{l\to\infty}\operatorname{\mathds{1}}_{E_l},
\qquad
\mathbb P(E)\le\liminf_{l\to\infty}\mathbb P(E_l)
\le C_r m^{-r},
\]
where the middle inequality is Fatou's lemma. This proves both inequalities
for arbitrary proper CDFs. The approximation takes place at each fixed $m$
and is used only in this proof; the estimators and bootstrap do not modify
any observed outcomes.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Condition on the fixed pairs. For any $y$, the variables
$e_{I_i^*}(y)-\bar e_m(y)$ are independent, centered, bounded by two, and have
conditional variance
\[
\frac1m\sum_{j=1}^m\{e_j(y)-\bar e_m(y)\}^2
\le\frac1m\sum_{j=1}^m e_j(y)^2\le\kappa_m.
\]
As $y$ varies, the vector $(e_1(y),\ldots,e_m(y))$ has at most $2m+1$
distinct values: it changes only at an observed $A_i$ or $B_i$.
At one representative threshold for each vector, apply
\eqref{cdf32:eq:bernstein} with variance bound $m\kappa_m$, bound two on
the centered summands, and $t=(r+3)\log m$. A union bound has failure
probability at most $2(2m+1)m^{-(r+3)}\le C_r m^{-r}$.
On its complement, division by $\sqrt m$ gives
\[
\sup_y\left|\frac1{\sqrt m}\sum_i
                       \{e_{I_i^*}(y)-\bar e_m(y)\}\right|
\le\sqrt{2\kappa_m(r+3)\log m}
      +\frac{4(r+3)\log m}{3\sqrt m}.
\]
This is \eqref{cdf32:eq:resampling-bound} with an enlarged $C_r$.
Ties only decrease the number of distinct vectors.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
